Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: &quot;Unshackled&quot; spending post retirement anyone?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →



I don’t really disagree with anything you said.

But the obvious caveat is that I don’t know if I’ll be in the 78% until I’ve made all my spending choices.
Maybe this apllies.
Perhaps a set of questions would help some.....

1. Will the persons goals and/or dreams be affected by lowering spending from 4% to X% ?
No - congratulations , you are done no need to go further
Yes - please proceed
2. If you begin spending at 4% do you also have a reasonable plan to decrease spending if there is a 'crisis' requiring it?
No - you are done , carry on
Yes - please proceed
3. If you begin spending at 4% with no crisis do you see any reason to pre-emptively cut back 'just in case" of a crisis later on?
Yes - ask yourself why
No - please proceed
4. If you get thru 4-5 years at 4% without a crisis do you think that you will need a full inflation adjustment each year?
Yes - please calculate this in detail for your use
No - please proceed
5. Do you feel the 'utility' of your additional spending is higher when younger?
No - please list the events/travel/activities in your plan grouped by decade
Yes - please proceed
6. Have you used the calculator called "Rich, Broke, or Dead" for at least an hour?
No - please consider the value of that review
Yes - great, you are done
Hope this helps
I want to talk about #4 the inflation adjustment.

The traditional SWR uses CPI as the inflation adjustment. But I have 2 problems with that.
1) I don't know a single person that increases how much they pull out of their portfolio based on CPI. Most people have a budget that they try to stay in and if CPI is 3%, they don't really feel it all that much for a few years and tend to keep a similar budget.

2) Person inflation is not the same is CPI. CPI includes things like home prices as a large percentage. Most of us own a home with no mortgage in retirement. insurance prices may go up, utilities may go up, but all in all, that portion of CPI does not affect us all that much. Even healthcare costs don't affect most of us as we will be on Medicare at that time. It is only late in life if long term care is needed will we see a our personal inflation may outpace CPI

My point is, CPI is used in all those calculations though most people tend to not increase spending by that statistic effectively lowering their withdrawal rate and increasing the odds that their portfolio will grow over time.

From ‘Stranger Things’ to ‘Rings of Power,’ We’re All Living in Jamie Campbell Bower’s Fantasy World

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After ‘Stranger Things,’ Bower is letting his guard down as Celeborn in ‘The Rings of Power’ Season 3 — and finding freedom in the process.

Jamie Campbell Bower seems surprised by the notion that he’s become the king of genre. It seems like a topic worth bringing up at the start of our conversation, though, and not just because he’s carved out some time from an increasingly packed schedule to talk about the latest high-profile project on his acting resume. The bigger reason is that Bower himself has always seemed like the type of actor who innately belongs in a realm that’s much more extraordinary than our own.

Even the briefest glance at his filmography immediately lends credence to that belief. After scoring his first on-screen role in Tim Burton’s silver-screen adaptation of Sweeney Todd, he hopped on the vampire train, appearing in three of five Twilight Saga films, then played a bad-boy Shadowhunter in the big-screen swing at The Mortal Instruments and later picked up a wizard’s wand for two Harry Potter movies. He nearly became part of Game of Thrones with a prequel series that would have preceded House of the Dragon — before HBO decided not to move forward with it. And countless words have been written about Netflix's Stranger Things, with Bower’s performance as the nightmare-inducing Vecna arguably transforming him into a worldwide phenomenon right alongside the Duffer Brothers' show itself. But the truth is that his journey to the fantastical started much earlier than that.

“If I'm to go right back to even before Sweeney Todd and being on-screen, my formative years were being on stage at my local theater and participating in productions like Alice in Wonderland,” Bower reflects. While his preteen enthusiasm for music and performance has certainly endured since then — there’s even an electric guitar casually displayed in the background of the office he’s calling from — he admits now that what called to him early on transcended any one medium.

“I suppose, in a way, I've always been drawn to those worlds. I've always been drawn to immersion into universes and finding a sense of beauty in them, regardless of what it is that I'm doing. It's the stepping outside and stepping into that has always interested me. Period. And that goes not just across acting, but other creative pursuits.”

It’s a more thoughtful response compared to the version that Bower admits he’s previously given — now, he has the years, experience, and perspective to be able to look back on a career largely made up of several big-name genre franchises and find the deeper meaning of it all.

“I remember getting asked questions like that when I was younger, and being flippant and off-the-cuff. ‘Oh, well, it's because I have long hair and I look like a sort of sickly Victorian child,’” he quips, before his expression turns more reflective. “But I think it's probably more just the fact that that's what my soul craves.”

Jamie Campbell Bower poses for his Collider Signature profile at Our Home Studios in Los Angeles on Oct. 12, 2026, in support of Season 3 of Prime Video’s “The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power.” (Photo by Gina Gizella Manning)

Jamie Campbell Bower poses for his Collider Signature profile at Our Home Studios in Los Angeles on Oct. 12, 2026, in support of Season 3 of Prime Video’s “The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power.” (Photo by Gina Gizella Manning)

As any fantasy or sci-fi lover can attest, the biggest stories start with the smallest kernel of an idea. Whether the world in question is made up of dragons or aliens, it all originated from a single mind. The adaptation process, on the other hand, which Bower has increasingly become familiar with over the most notable stretch of his career, represents the exact opposite. By the time the last Twilight movie or the Stranger Things series finale had premiered, the end result was the product of countless individuals working together across different departments to create something that the London-born actor describes as the purest form of fun. Within that collaboration, Bower shares, is where he’s most comfortable existing, especially in the wake of an adolescence where he’s previously admitted he often felt like an outsider.

“Performing was always this space where I felt freest,” he divulges, before offering up something that’s even more revealing, given his performance history. “It's also been the space where I felt the most terrified.”

Some of that fear, Bower expands, sits in the pressure that can come from joining an already established world — take Season 4 of Stranger Things, or the newest installment of a major Amazon fantasy adaptation that we’re dancing around discussing before getting into the nitty-gritty story details.

“Being on a film set’s very scary at times — particularly when you're young or when you’re stepping into a world that is loved or known.” Alongside that weight, however, Bower readily reflects on how his past experiences on fantastical film sets have actually been a form of creative escape. “Maybe this is art in general sometimes, but more in these worlds, I feel a greater sense of, dare I say it, emotional safety. There's that warmth. There's that strong sense of blending in oneself. That, I think, is the thing for me. Because I go into a room like that and, ‘Oh, I'm home! I know where I am.’”

That hasn’t always been the case for him on every set, he alludes, but it’s hard not to notice the instances where Bower has been afforded the most freedom to show what he’s capable of — even behind extensively intricate makeup and prosthetics — are also the moments when it’s impossible to look away from his performance. Beyond acting, that aforementioned desire for complete creative immersion seems to have evolved into a philosophy for everyday life.

“I'm a big believer that we can all create our own worlds, whether it be in our own writing or how we decorate, what we choose to consume.”

Since we’re already on the subject of his filmography, though, it makes sense that Bower then moves to shout out those whose contributions to these larger-than-life worlds he’s been able to play in often go unsung.

“There are also other people involved in that sense of belonging, within the creation of those worlds,” he admits. “I, as an actor, have my own experiences of that, but there's also the other beautiful, probably more important work that goes on within that is so hands-on.” He immediately acknowledges the hair and makeup departments that have aided in his past screen transformations — and specifically name-drops five-time Emmy-winning prosthetics designer Barrie Gower, who spearheaded the creation of Vecna through largely practical effects, as well as Alex Sherrod, the lead sculptor for Stranger Things’ final season.

“This is a space where the freedom to be creative across the board is so apparent for all of us, and that's such a pleasurable thing to be around because you're seeing people do the thing that they love. It’s existed in their brains, and it’s been percolating, and all of a sudden it's manifesting into a reality in order to build a world, and that is so freeing and pure.”

In case you haven’t picked up on it already, the ability to find community and even sometimes family on a project is something Bower inherently holds dear — so perhaps it’s not surprising at all that his time on Stranger Things, which he describes as full of “beautiful memories” filming in Atlanta, is ultimately what led him down the path to entering Middle-earth.

When Bower first learned that he’d been cast in Season 3 of The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power, he burst into tears.

That’s not hyperbole. It’s a variation of a story Bower himself has been telling in the months following the official confirmation of his casting. Not only did he, by his own admission, start weeping, but he also had to pull over — he was driving at the time — and call his younger brother, Samuel, to share the good news. While he’s alluded to the existence of FaceTime screenshots that confirm the intensity of his reaction, he’s also insisted that the video in question will never see the light of day.

Still, it seems worth resurrecting the anecdote during our own conversation, if only to ask: What kind of significance does J.R.R. Tolkien’s world hold for Bower, and why did it conjure such a profound swirl of emotion for him when he found out he was going to be a part of it? As it happens, we’re the same age, so of course we both have equally vivid memories of watching — and rewatching — Peter Jackson’s legendary adaptation of the classic fantasy trilogy.

“Those first three films were, have, and always will be the movies that I put on as I'm lying sideways on the couch with a blanket over me on a rainy October, November, December, January, February, March… it depends on where you are. If you're in England, it could be fucking any time,” Bower quips. “But they hold so much peace and joy and light within that heart space for me.”

That initial introduction to Tolkien came by the time he was entering his teenage years, he adds — and on the heels of mostly “playing Power Rangers,” so there was no pretending to wield swords as Aragorn or a bow and arrow as Legolas. Yet watching Tolkien’s story unfold onscreen through Jackson’s lens kept bringing him back to reflect on the deeper themes underneath, and that ultimately made Middle-earth a very intriguing playground for an actor continually looking to immerse himself in the otherworldly.

“I'm curious about the possibility — fuck it, I’ll be more confident,” Bower laughs. “I’m curious about the probability that when Tolkien created that world, it was so imbued with this sense of love and beauty, and yes, of course, the battle between darkness and light. But even Tolkien, and I'll probably fudge this, said something along the lines of, ‘Good can often do evil and doesn't realize that it's doing it.’ There is a certain nuance to that.”

Years later, during the production of Stranger Things, Bower couldn’t help but notice that one of his fellow artists had a foothold in the latest adaptation of Tolkien’s writings. During the hours-long process of prosthetic application required to turn him from disarmingly handsome to utterly terrifying, he attempted to pick Gower’s brain about working on Rings of Power as often as possible. Gower’s answer — describing his experience on set as being akin to “family” — seemingly told Bower everything he needed to know, and by the time the offer to audition for the Prime Video series finally came his way, he embraced his first hunch.

“I always tend to go with a gut feeling when I receive something, and it's been through trial and error,” Bower admits, with a laugh. “But I remember when I received the sides for the show, and having watched the first season and being in the middle of watching Season 2 at the same time, and working with Barrie Gower, there was this pervasive sense of deep emotional connection, and it did feel like something else. It felt like something that I hadn't been experiencing for some time.”

After an initial audition, which Bower taped with his brother’s help, as well as a chemistry read with star Morfydd Clark (more on that later), the good news came through. As it happens, those initial tears of joy were only the beginning of what defined the first three weeks of production on Season 3 — where, as he divulged via roundtable at San Diego Comic-Con, his inner child was “kicking and screaming with glee” at the realization of the adventure he was about to embark on.

“It's that feeling of what seems to have been baked into the Lord of the Rings cake,” Bower expands with me now, “and from the discussions that you and I are having, I'm going to make an assumption that you feel the same, but that's the thing that grabs us. It's like looking at a painting. There are some paintings that I look at, and I’m like, ‘That’s beautiful, but it’s not for me. I don’t get it.’ And there are other pieces of art that aren't necessarily beautiful, but I'm like, ‘Oh my God, this is moving me in such a way that I have no choice but to just stare at it,’ and that's what this world did to me and did for me.”

When Bower was finally announced as a member of The Rings of Power’s Season 3 cast, the role itself was still a secret, but anyone with eyes could have guessed, based on bone structure alone, that he would be playing an Elf. More than that, he’d be playing the one Elf whose absence from the narrative has been the subject of hot debate. Within the massive scope of Prime Video’s adaptation, Celeborn holds significance as the husband of Galadriel, played by Clark, with the question of his whereabouts hanging over the story from the very first episode. Although he is originally presumed dead after the events of the war that resulted in the defeat of the Dark Lord Morgoth, Season 3 reveals that Celeborn has actually been alive the entire time — though not necessarily well, after spending an age imprisoned and apart from his wife.

"Of course there was a fear, but the walls were down, and that's where I wanted Celeborn to be."

For Bower, the role of Celeborn represented a stark change of pace after Stranger Things’ official series wrap, particularly in the makeup chair. Although he’d certainly ditched Vecna’s prosthetics in order to play the character’s original human incarnation, Henry Creel, he wanted to bring something new to his performance in The Rings of Power, stripping away any sense of artifice or manipulation in favor of something that actually proved even more intimidating to inhabit.

“There was always something else that [Henry] wanted, regardless of what it was that he was doing,” Bower explains. “So a lot of the performance was about performing to the other actor that I was in front of. ‘Don't see my intention. I know what I want from you, but I'm not going to show you what I want from you.’ Whereas, for Celeborn, and this idea of sensitivity and vulnerability, I was like, “I'm going to approach this character with a total open-heart space. What he is saying, what he wants, what he is, will be what you see.’”

Admittedly, it still takes a minute before Celeborn and Galadriel’s emotionally fraught reunion happens in the show’s present-day timeline. Season 3 picks up after a five-year time jump, where the War of the Elves and Sauron is well underway. The new Dark Lord, who’s taken over from his predecessor, has already made some decisive moves in conquering Middle-earth, with only a handful of regions — including Númenor and Rivendell — still left to stand against the encroaching evil. If it feels like everyone has been touched by Sauron’s corruption in some form in Season 3, that’s intentional, and Bower didn’t want to shy away from emphasizing that his own character has been put through the wringer while in captivity.

“Celeborn has been through the works. He has suffered, and I wanted to show that suffering, because if he buries it and he presents the false version of himself, there’s no connection. And in the face of this suffering, Celeborn can hold space for the pain. He can choose not to make everyone else suffer, whereas with Henry, he’s like, ‘Well, I suffer, therefore everyone else suffers.’” Even in this snapshot examination of the two characters, both of whom happen to share his face, it’s evident that Stranger Things’ most iconic villain still holds a special place in Bower’s heart.

“I love Henry, and I love the little boy inside of him, but there's a great sense of protection around him that he has created. With Celeborn, I wanted walls gone. I wanted it all to be there and accessible and witnessed.”

The decision to embrace more vulnerability, though, was accompanied by the spike of fear that Bower’s had before — but it led to its own epiphany once he was finally on set at Shepperton Studios, where The Rings of Power’s third season was primarily filmed.

“I remember the first day of shooting and being so terrified. So, so terrified. And then there was this moment where it just dropped in. I have quite visceral reactions to things — if I know something feels good within me, I'm like, ‘Ah, yes!’ I remember being on set, being like, ‘Ah, yes!’ and then seeing members of the crew look at me and being like, ‘Sorry.’” Bower chuckles at the recollection, but the assertion that follows is as unapologetic as any other instance where our conversation has referenced his bigger shows of emotion. “Of course there was a fear, but the walls were down, and that's where I wanted Celeborn to be.”

The below contains spoilers for The Rings of Power Season 3 premiere.

As refreshingly in touch with his feelings as Bower’s Celeborn is, that trait might not have been nearly as successful without the right chemistry onscreen. When the reveal of his role was first announced, the actor was quick to describe his version of the character as a “lover-boy” — arguably a far cry from the reserved elder and statesman he becomes by the events of Tolkien’s novel trilogy. Yet The Rings of Power Season 3 practically gifts Bower with the perfect opportunity to show off a more sensitive side, with an opening flashback that jumps back to the very first moment Celeborn and Galadriel laid eyes on each other. Bower says his own connection with Clark was simultaneously instantaneous, but certainly began — at least on his end — from a place of professional reverence.

“It's no secret that I have been a fan of Morfydd’s work for a very long time.” How big a fan, you ask? “I mean, I have merchandise with her on it.” He goes on to cite Clark’s acclaimed performance in Rose Glass’s psychological horror film Saint Maud as an influence on his own work. Apparently, it only took one look during their chemistry read, the final stage in Bower’s audition process, for the two actors to forge their own bond.

“I was in the knowledge of what I wanted to bring, which was this rawness,” he reflects. “The moment we started chemistry reading together, there was this instant connection, this instant sense of safety. I've discovered that in my own artistic journey, that feeling of safety is so important with all the creative people that I'm working with. So we locked eyes, and it was like, ‘We're here.’”

In hindsight, Bower adds that even if his test with Clark hadn’t led to him booking the role of Celeborn, getting to act opposite someone he’s admired for so long was a worthwhile experience on its own. Fortunately, that’s not what happened next. “I genuinely remember leaving the chemistry read and jumping on the phone and calling one of my delightful, long-suffering agents, and saying, ‘I had the best time. If this is all it is, I'm so grateful. I'm so happy.’ Then, of course, a week later I get the phone call, and I'm driving along and crying.”

When Celeborn and Galadriel first cross paths, the ensuing meet-cute plays out through an unexpected amount of physicality and stuntwork for both actors. What Bower refers to as a “tête-à-tête fight” feels perfectly fitting for the warrior Galadriel is at this point in the story, particularly after two seasons of heavy action. The fact that she’d rather practice the sword alone, in the woods, attracts the attention of Celeborn, who finds himself torn between a weariness about war and an inability to fully reintegrate himself into a peaceful Elven society. When the two begin sparring with each other, the moment evolves from a playfully flirty encounter into the beginnings of an epic romance.

"It's no secret that I have been a fan of Morfydd’s work for a very long time."

“There's such a beauty to all of that movement,” Bower praises. “It's an intentional way of fighting, and it’s so Elven, and it's just fluid and dreamy and powerful and strong.” His desire to preserve the elegance of the entire sequence resulted in close collaboration with Rings of Power stunt coordinator Roy Taylor as well as series director Charlotte Brändström, who helms some of Season 3’s most pivotal episodes. Because Bower was so intent on nailing such a defining scene for his character, it led to a brief surge of disappointment when he convinced himself he hadn’t initially done so.

“I remember going home and being upset about the fact that I didn't feel like I'd gotten something right on set,” the actor explains, before expanding on the moment in question — when Galadriel challenges Celeborn to graze her with his sword and initially believes she’s escaped unscathed, only to find he’s successfully cut off a lock of her hair, which she allows him to keep. “I watched the playback, and I was like, ‘No, it's not beautiful enough,’” Bower recounts, “and Charlotte was like, ‘It's fine, Jamie. Don't worry.’ I saw the edit, and I was like, ‘She’s absolutely right. It is fine.’ But I very much wanted to keep that beauty throughout the whole fight.”

What proved even more disarming than crossing swords was Celeborn and Galadriel’s choreographed party dance, which Bower says handed him and Clark another means of connection — primarily because they approached it with a shared degree of reluctance.

“I did dance at school, but I don't love to dance, and haven't danced on camera for years. I think the last time I danced on camera was a film I made with Roland Emmerich, called Anonymous. I don’t think I even danced in that; I just had a lesson. And of course, that’s a very exposing place to be. You’re both like, ‘Well, I’m shit at this, so let’s see how this goes.’” Bower laughs. “But I think that helped us to build that relationship — because within that, it was just kind of like play again.”

His on-screen romance with Clark almost feels like a whirlwind on its own — in one moment the characters are learning each other’s first names, and in the next they’re planning out their future and the life they want to have together after the fighting is over. Yet none of it would have been possible without what Bower likens to an immediate esteem between co-stars that only strengthened during Season 3’s production.

“It was there instantly, and then every step that we took towards the world of it and towards building it was another step forward,” he says. “I think there's also a truth to having watched the films when I was younger and seeing Galadriel, and being like, ‘Oh my God! She’s amazing!’ You carry that reverence with you, too. I don't know what that is — by luck, by design, by potential manifestation, but it was just there. It was just in us.”

While Celeborn’s introduction in The Rings of Power is a pretty suave one, all things considered, he’s a very different Elf when Season 3 returns to him centuries later. Gone is the character who once fought with beauty and elegance, and in its place is someone who’s been forced to abandon all of that in favor of simply living to fight another day. Behind the scenes, it led to frequent discussions between actor and stunt team about how best to represent that deterioration of form whenever Celeborn had reason to pick up a weapon.

“This character has been imprisoned for so long,” Bower explains, discussing his conversations with Taylor about the biggest difference between Celeborn’s past and present approach to combat. “I wonder if there's a world in which the beauty of form within his fighting is, at this point, no longer necessary because it's become about survival.”

Finding the best way to portray that devolution was an experimental process on set, the actor adds, recalling one sequence in particular featuring an imprisoned Celeborn “when we went to 11 on it and had to kind of dial it back to seven.” The specifics are less important than what it led to: the revelation that Bower had to cross that invisible line for the first time to know exactly where it was. “We had to go there to bring it back,” he admits, before launching into a longer explanation about the Elf lord’s overall feelings about violence.

“It's not something that he celebrates. It's not something that he's going to be overjoyed about. I remember his voice kind of popping up in my head before we would do these scenes: ‘If I have to, then I must. It’s not that I don’t want to, but if I have to.’ That has been a cool fundamental belief that I discovered within creating him. He’s adept and good at what he can do, but would prefer not to.”

Despite the decision to embrace less physical restraint for Celeborn over time, Bower’s own voice, which has a distinct resonance, feels perfectly suited to a character with centuries of wisdom to draw from. Yet even within the world of The Rings of Power, the Elves have a mellifluous way of speaking that’s born from their own language. Finding the best pitch for Celeborn may not have been as demanding as preparing to deliver Vecna’s chilling dialogue in Stranger Things, for example, but it was still a matter of striking the right balance.

“There were takes that we did in that first meet-cute between Galadriel and Celeborn where my pitch was so high. I was like, ‘I wonder how youthful we can make him at this point.’ And it didn’t work.” Bower chuckles. “So, we had to find that middle ground.”

While Celeborn couldn’t exactly start from a place of innocence, it was still essential for his voice to be equally reflective of his Season 3 arc while also hinting at the co-ruler of Lothlórien he will ultimately become. It’s a significant part of what Bower describes as the overall journey he went on with the character.

“The experiences that he goes through — particularly, and I would say most importantly, this season — really transform the way he ends up sounding. It was always going to be about having this ethereal nature and this lyricism and this poetic kind of being to him, and a sense of beauty, but it was also about exploring the journey of innocence into suffering, into finding hope, into finding light, into suffering again, and getting into this space of wisdom. And within that wisdom — and I've even sent emails more recently to the creators about where I found him now — it’s about finding that stillness, finding that balance and knowledge.”

When I ask whether Bower’s own musical background proved helpful in figuring out how Celeborn would sound, he admits that his experience there — first at London’s National Youth Music Theatre, later with the now-disbanded group Counterfeit, and now in both collaborative and solo performance efforts — has helped him develop a stronger understanding of his body’s most unmistakable instrument and what it’s capable of.

In the same breath, however, Bower confirms that no amount of planning is a proper substitute for what can emerge spontaneously, sometimes when it’s least expected. It happened with Vecna, whose hair-raising cadence Bower initially envisioned in the vein of Freddy Krueger before drawing inspiration from Hellraiser’s Pinhead, and it happened again with The Rings of Power’s most highly anticipated new character.

“There is also something that happens when you’re creating, and I'm sure you experience this as a writer, too. You can do all the notes, and you can do all the pre-thinking and all the kitchen table work, as we like to call it, but there's also a point in time where you have to be like, ‘You're going to do what you're going to do, and you're going to fly and exist in your way.’ And then he lands.”

There’s no possibility of us delving any further into The Rings of Power’s third season — partly because only four episodes will have premiered as of this writing, and partly because Bower very gallantly wants to avoid divulging any twists I haven’t screened yet. Since our time together is almost at an end, now seems like the right time to inquire about what’s next for him, beyond Middle-earth.

After years in worlds containing supernatural creatures of all sorts, you’d think Bower might easily be drawn to something more contemporary, but there’s actually one specific genre that he’s willing to admit he doesn’t think he has the chops for.

“I just had a conversation with my team the other day about the things that are interesting me and the things that I want to avoid, and comedy is always the thing that I'm so desperate to avoid. It's such a unique, specific talent that some people have, and I'm not that guy. I just don't have that. Some people can paint beautifully; I'm not a painter. That's just not in the skills that I've been fortunate enough to have,” he concedes, flashing a grin that ends up somewhere between self-effacing and thoroughly charming. “So comedy's always the thing that we’re kind of like, ‘Eh, we're okay.’”

That doesn’t mean he doesn’t feel some kind of pull toward a more grounded tale, and there’s no hesitation in him when he confirms as such. “There's always been a part of me that's wanted to do more of what we in England call the ‘kitchen sink drama,’ one that is complex and deeply human. That would be really interesting to me.”

"Comedy is always the thing that I'm so desperate to avoid."

Amidst whatever stories are still lying in wait for Bower to put his unique stamp on, his biggest future pursuit is his first-ever solo concert in London next year. He privately breaks the news here, not knowing that by the time the announcement goes out to fans, tickets will sell out impressively fast. Although performing solely as himself has the potential to resurrect some of that fear we’ve been talking about, it also represents yet another avenue for Bower to embrace the freedom that simply creating can provide. It’s a lesson he still finds himself navigating from time to time — with the occasional guidance from those who have been in the business a bit longer.

“Two weeks ago, I sat next to a musician in a very famous band, and I asked them, ‘After all these years, it's a business for you, and it's how you pay your mortgage and put your kids through school. How do you still find the joy in it?’ And they said to me, ‘The first thing I do when I get into the room with the guys, I say, ‘Do you want to play?’ I said, ‘Fuck. It's just that fucking simple.’ That's all it is. In other creative pursuits in my life, I've been so used to being the person that has to oversee everything to the point of it being quite consuming — unaware that it's consuming, but it being consuming.”

As Bower prepares to return to the Alexandra Palace Theatre, it’s tempting to ask whether any of his most recent stage performances — like last year’s one-night reprisal of the role of Henry Creel in Stranger Things: The First Shadow — had anything to do with it. Instead, he brings up a January email from Biffy Clyro frontman Simon Neil, which included an offer to perform alongside the band at Finsbury Park. But even that opportunity very nearly didn’t come to pass when Bower’s fear briefly took over.

“I was so struck by a certain terror that it took me three months to reply to his email — and bless him, when I did reply positively, he was still positive about the opportunity! But I got to that show, and I saw everybody working at the top level, doing their thing, and that nothing needed touching.

“I didn't have to do anything,” Bower muses, “but I can't lie and tell you that my brain wasn’t like, ‘Well, I've got my monitor mix, but what if I need this?’ And I was just like, ‘Chill. Just let it happen and let it breathe and let it be.’ And I did, and I just had the most incredible experience.”

There’s no doubt that night became an important reminder of what Bower wants to strive for with his own show, the details of which are still largely under wraps — but even the tease he’s willing to drop now feels perfectly aligned with his biggest artistic goals. “It’s really about a reconnection to that childlike sense of wonder and love and hope and safety — and yeah, weirdness. Of course, weirdness.”

Regardless of which realm he ends up playing in, Bower’s determined to have fun there, but he’s also learning to be content with not having control over every single detail. On a personal level, it turns out that just means knowing when to let other artists help him build the most immersive version of his own world.

“That's what I'm going back to now — that sense of letting go and letting things exist, and… fuck it, stepping back into my fantasy,” Bower declares, with a mischievous smile. “There you go. You're welcome.”

Dylan Sprouse, Barbie Ferreira and Tanner Buchanan are heading to the Hamptons together for “The Sleeping Dogs of Southampton,” a psychological thriller set inside a “dangerous world of sex, money and obsession,” according to the description.

Directed by David Lipper (“Neglected”) and written by Peter James Cooper, the “The Sleeping Dogs of Southampton” is heading to the American Film Market, where Highland Film Group is launching worldwide sales. Principal photography recently wrapped. Related Stories 'The Peanut Butter Falcon' Breakout Zack Gottsagen and Anna Enger Ritch to Star in 'A Reason to Stay' From Director Rippin Sindher (EXCLUSIVE) Connor Storrie in Talks to Play Villain Opposite Ryan Gosling in Marvel's 'Ghost Rider'

Alongside Sprouse (“Beautiful Disaster”), Ferreira (“Euphoria”) and Buchanan (“Cobra Kai”), the film will also star Jack Kilmer (“The Nice Guys”), Rainey Qualley (“Shut In”), Bianca Ghezzi (“The Idol”) and Ethan Cutkowsky (“Shameless”).

In “The Sleeping Dogs of Southampton,” as per the synopsis, ruthless Wall Street rainmaker Dorsey Grace (Sprouse) knows how to close a deal — and get inside your head. Over scotch with business associates, he brags about a wild past hookup, leading rising and hyper-competitive banker Henry Barrett (Buchanan) to suspect the unnamed woman was his wife, Lindsay (Ferreira). Over a seductive summer among Manhattan’s moneyed elite (Qualley, Kilmer, Ghezzi), their sprawling estates and lavish pool parties of the Hamptons, suspicion turns to obsession. Drawn into a world of sex, money and carefully guarded secrets, Henry becomes locked in a lethal game of corporate warfare and psychological chess as he seeks to destroy a man who always seems one move ahead.

“’The Sleeping Dogs of Southampton’ is an irresistibly sexy thriller that takes audiences into a glamorous world where money, power and desire become a dangerous combination,” said Highland Film Group CEO Arianne Fraser.

All Deku-Stick Bag upgrade locations in Ocarina of Time

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If you love using Deku Sticks as a weapon in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake, you'll want to upgrade your Deku-Stick Bag to carry even more sticks. You can do this pretty early in the game, but this isn't really necessary to beat the main story. Most people will beat the game without upgrading their stick bag, but if you want to do everything there is to do, don't skip out on these bag upgrades.

Below, we explain where to find all the Deku-Stick Bag upgrades in the Ocarina of Time remake.

You can unlock these upgrades as child Link (which works out, since you can't use Deku Sticks as adult Link), but if you forgot to nab them as a kid and are feeling too lazy to swap back, you can also unlock these upgrades as adult Link.

You can find both of these upgrades in the Lost Woods, though you will need the Skull Mask to get the second one.

Originally, your Deku Stick Bag can hold up to 10 Deku Sticks, but these upgrades will let you carry 10 more each, for a total of 30 sticks.

For the first bag upgrade, head into the Lost Woods from Kokiri Forest and then immediately head to the left. This will put you in the room with the Skull Kid. Ignore them and take another left, which will send you down a wooded pathway.

At the end of the pathway, there's a Business Scrub. Defeat it by reflecting its nuts back at it, and it'll beg for your forgiveness and offer to sell you a Deku-Stick Bag upgrade for 40 rupees.

For the second upgrade, you'll need to follow another specific path when you enter the Lost Woods from Kokiri Forest: right, left, right, left, and then left again.

This takes you to a clearing with some greenery surrounding a hole in the ground. If you come here as young Link, there'll be two Business Scrubs. (There's also a soft soil mound nearby if you want to plant a bean or find a Skulltula.)

Drop into the hole, and you'll see a sign that says, "Stand on the stage and prepare your face for an evaluation." Put on your Skull Mask and then stand on the stump. The Deku Scrubs will be so excited to see you in that mask. After they swarm you for a little bit, a bigger Deku Scrub will pop out of the ground and give you another Deku-Stick Bag upgrade for free.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake is a little bit different than the original game, so if you need guides to help you through, we don’t blame you. We have walkthroughs for all the dungeons, from the Deku Tree to Ganondorf's Castle. We also have guides for the sillier, in-between parts of the game, like when you have to find Richard (the dog) or Cuccos.

For those going for 100% completion, we also have guides on where to find every Piece of Heart and every Gold Skulltula token.

Johnson & Johnson Sees a Path to Double-Digit Growth by 2030. Is the Dividend King a Buy?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The business has undergone major changes in recent years, and these might pay off over the medium term.

Johnson & Johnson (JNJ -0.35%) has made significant business changes over the past few years. Perhaps the most notable was the company's decision to spin off its consumer health division into a stand-alone, publicly traded company. The drugmaker made this move partly because consumer health had become a drag on sales growth, and it bet that eventually, top-line growth would improve as a result of this spinoff. Now, management thinks that Johnson & Johnson could achieve double-digit sales growth by 2030. Does this guidance make the stock a buy? Let's find out.

In the first nine months of the year, Johnson & Johnson reported $74.8 billion in sales, up 7.4% year over year. That's a strong performance for the pharmaceutical giant, and even more impressive given the biosimilar competition for Stelara. This immunology drug lost U.S. patent exclusivity in 2025. Stelara was a meaningful growth driver. In fact, even after losing exclusivity, it remains one of Johnson & Johnson's top-selling drugs.

Through Sept. 30, it generated $2.1 billion in sales, down 56.4% year over year. Excluding Stelara, Johnson & Johnson's total revenue through Sept. 30 grew by about 12.2% year over year. Looking forward, Johnson & Johnson could face more patent cliffs over the next five years. Darzalex, a cancer drug and the company's best-selling product, could lose U.S. patent exclusivity by the end of the decade.

To achieve its goal of double-digit sales growth, Johnson & Johnson will have to find new drugs to navigate upcoming patent expirations. Fortunately, the company has made progress along those lines. In recent years, Johnson & Johnson has launched several new products to diversify its pipeline, boost top-line growth, and overcome patent cliffs. The company's newest drugs include Icotyde, an oral pill for plaque psoriasis. Others include Inlexzo and Lazcluze, both of which are cancer drugs, and Imaavy, which is approved to treat myasthenia gravis, a chronic autoimmune condition.

Johnson & Johnson has estimated that all four of these products have peak sales potential exceeding $5 billion. These are projections, not results. But it's not hard to see the potential of these medicines. For instance, consider Icotyde, which belongs to the IL-23 pathway inhibitor category, a group of medicines that treat various diseases by blocking immune signals that cause excessive inflammation.

Most medicines in this group require injections. Icotyde became the first oral IL-23 pathway inhibitor to earn approval for plaque psoriasis. Since it offers a much more convenient option, it could carve out a niche in this large market, as it earns additional indications elsewhere. Multibillion-dollar peak sales don't seem outside this medicine's reach. Further, Johnson & Johnson has other highly promising candidates.

One of them is Milvexian, an investigational anticoagulant that could solve one problem today's anticoagulants face: it could reduce the significant bleeding risk they pose. Lastly, even within its medical device segment, Johnson & Johnson has made progress.

For instance, the company has earned U.S. clearance for the Ottava. This robotic-assisted surgery device will allow it to tap into the underpenetrated robotic surgery market -- Johnson & Johnson has estimated that fewer than 8% of eligible soft tissue surgeries are currently performed robotically.

Johnson & Johnson can see revenue growth rates increase as sales of newer products ramp up, even as it continues to face generic and biosimilar competition for older drugs.

Johnson & Johnson looks well-positioned to achieve its goal of double-digit sales growth by 2030, and there are several other reasons to buy the stock. Here are two of them. First, the company has taken significant steps toward resolving the thousands of talc-related lawsuits it has faced for years. A recent proposed settlement would have Johnson & Johnson pay $5.5 billion to plaintiffs, with the first payment of no more than $3 billion due in 2027 and no additional payment due before 2028.

For a company that could generate over $100 billion in annual sales this year, that's a fairly small price to pay to put thousands of lawsuits that have tarnished its public image in the rearview mirror. Second, Johnson & Johnson remains a fantastic income stock. The company's streak of 64 consecutive annual payout increases makes it a Dividend King, a status that requires at least 50 straight annual dividend increases. Dividend seekers should seriously consider adding this stock to their portfolios and holding it for the long term.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 835%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 208% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

Kim Kardashian’s Former Beverly Hills Tuscan Villa From Early Seasons of ‘Keeping Up With the Kardashians’ Hits the Market for $8.5 Million

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Kim Kardashian’s former Beverly Hills home has hit the market, with an asking price of $8.495 million for a piece of reality television history.

The five-bedroom, five-bathroom home was featured heavily in the earliest seasons of “Keeping Up With the Kardashians.” Remember when Kris Jenner and Khloé Kardashian went out on the town, and after a few too many margaritas, decided to toilet-paper the Tuscan-style villa?

Fortunately, the defaced rose bushes have been cleaned up since that 2013 episode.

Kim purchased the 4,027-square-foot home for $3.4 million in 2010. Three years later, she sold it fully-furnished, and it went for $5.12 million after a bidding war, according to Robb Report.

Later, in 2024, Kim visited the property with Khloé and celebrity broker Josh Altman, to find that it was still nearly exactly as she’d left it. Recognizable details such as a “KK” logo on the wall of her iconic glam room and her custom bedding were still there, prompting the star to share a tour of the time capsule on her Snapchat profile.

Today, the famous family’s connection to the estate is more of an “if you know, you know” situation. It’s undergone a complete transformation by AD100 designer Sebastian Zuchowicki, and now boasts a more sleek, contemporary look.

With that said, many of the structural features that made the home a gem in the first place are still present.

There’s plenty of privacy, thanks to the gorgeous gates and lush landscaping surrounding the property. The front entrance is still as dramatic as ever, with a two-story foyer leading the way to the formal living and dining rooms. Both spaces have dedicated fireplaces, as well as French doors that connect the interior to the tranquil outside space.

The gourmet kitchen has been redone in a European country style, with custom cabinetry and a spacious center island. Charming windows line one wall, while a double-sided fireplace connects the breakfast room and lounge. New, top-of-the-line appliances have also been added.

The primary suite is certainly luxurious, with a fireside seating area, two fabulous and generously-sized walk-in closets, and a spa-inspired Italian-tiled bathroom that features double vanities, a soothing shower, and a unique soaking tub.

Additionally, the second floor also includes three more en suite bedrooms, which each have a distinct personality. From floral wallpaper to chic striped walls, these rooms manage to be neutral and statement-making at the same time.

The home is perfectly primed for entertaining, especially considering the private theater.

Indoor-outdoor living is encouraged, with a covered fireplace lounge and a fully equipped outdoor kitchen.

The resort-style pool and spa makes the backyard feel like a true getaway. There’s also a tranquil fountain, a hillside sun terrace, a dedicated bathroom, and a fire pit. A gazebo and cabana serves as a spot to watch television without being cooped up inside.

The gourmet kitchen is appointed with custom cabinetry, premium appliances, a spacious center island, and a double-sided fireplace connecting the breakfast room and lounge. A private theater provides an additional space for entertainment.

Today, Kim holds an impressive collection of properties in California and Idaho. Most notably, she’s spent years renovating her $60 million Hidden Hills home. The home has been undergoing major construction since 2024, and during a recent episode of “The Kardashians,” it was revealed that the Skims founder is creating an 8,000-square-foot closet.

Kim continues to share her real estate updates with viewers, and during the Nov. 5 episode of the family’s Hulu show, she purchased a new Palm Springs, CA, estate on what appeared to be a whim.

The Kardashian-Jenners have a series of homes in La Quinta, with hidden pathways connecting the properties. When Kim realized the house located next door to Kylie Jenner’s abode was up for grabs, she sneaked onto the property to see if she liked it better than her existing home.

Later in the episode, Khloé confirmed that her older sister had, in fact, made the purchase.

Hannah Chambers is a senior celebrity and lifestyle reporter who joined Realtor.com in 2026. She has been writing entertainment and lifestyle content since 2016, with her work appearing in Cosmopolitan, BravoTV, NYLON, Bustle, Elite Daily, and The Zoe Report, among other publications. Hannah received a bachelor’s degree in journalism from The University of Maine. In her spare time, she loves to chat about pop culture and is a major fan of reality TV.

8 facts about seasonal affective disorder, from symptoms to treatment, per the NIMH

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

SAD is clinical depression that follows the seasons. See 8 facts from the NIMH on its symptoms, causes, treatments and research.

Plenty of people feel more tired and less cheerful once the days get short, and most of them never think of it as a health problem. The real question is when a low season stops being a passing mood and becomes an illness that needs care. NIH News in Health reports that SAD, short for seasonal affective disorder, is a form of clinical depression tied to the time of year. It belongs with the depression doctors treat at any time of year and deserves the same seriousness, not the shrug people give a gloomy week. Telling a seasonal slump apart from a seasonal illness decides which kind of help fits.

Doctors know far more about how to treat SAD than about what causes it. Researchers point to a mix of influences acting together and have not identified any one exact cause, according to HealthDay. Much of the practical advice about SAD therefore rests on what has been seen to work, not on a settled account of why it works. Researchers can still name the conditions that tend to set off each form of the disorder. Kelly Rohan, a psychologist at the University of Vermont, tells NIH News in Health that shorter days set off the winter pattern, while excessive heat and humidity can prompt the summer one. Anyone deciding what to do about SAD is working from partial knowledge, and that makes a clinician's judgment more useful than a plan a person builds alone.

The National Institute of Mental Health set out to explain seasonal affective disorder to the public, from what the condition is and who tends to develop it to what may be driving it in the brain, how it is treated and how people can take part in research. Millions of Americans are estimated to have SAD. Doctors have treated its winter pattern with light since the 1980s, yet the summer pattern has no treatments designed specifically for it. The useful lesson for a reader is to recognize SAD as a medical condition and raise it early with a professional, not to work through its symptoms as a checklist for diagnosing oneself.

Most people picture seasonal affective disorder as a problem of short, dark winter afternoons, but the condition is defined by the season it keeps returning with, and that season can fall at either end of the year. The familiar form matches the popular image so closely that its other name is simply winter depression, according to the National Institute of Mental Health. The second form takes its name from its own season in the same way and is called summer depression. A person whose depression returns every summer has the same disorder as a person whose depression returns every winter. In each case the name tells a person when the episodes come, and that timing is what makes the illness seasonal.

The summer pattern is the form people are least likely to recognize, because it runs against the winter image and is also the less common of the two. That makes it easy for someone to explain away a low summer as something other than SAD. A mood that sinks every summer deserves the same attention as one that sinks every winter, because the season doesn't change what the condition is. On the question of treatment, "Talk to your doctor," Kelly Rohan, a psychologist at the University of Vermont, told NIH News in Health. People whose low mood arrives with summer have as much reason to follow that advice as people whose low mood arrives with winter.

Knowing the definition turns a low that returns on schedule, in either season, into something to raise with a doctor rather than something to wait out.

The clearest sign of SAD is how long a change in mood and behavior lasts, not any one feeling. People with SAD have symptoms for about 4−5 months each year, according to the National Institute of Mental Health. That stretches past a single season and lasts far longer than a rough week. Anyone wondering about SAD has to look at the full stretch of months, and one gloomy morning tells them almost nothing.

Many signs of winter-pattern SAD are changes in the body and in daily habits rather than feelings of sadness. NCCIH reports that people with this form have low energy and pull away from others. Many of them also oversleep, overeat, crave carbohydrates and gain weight. People can easily write these changes off as ordinary winter habits, and any one of them alone proves little. A reader who recognizes the whole set in themselves should take it to a clinician and not treat it as a self-diagnosis.

Anyone who checks only for the winter signs can miss SAD that comes with warm weather. People with the summer pattern have a different set of signs, including a poor appetite and weight loss, and they often feel restless, agitated and anxious. Friends and doctors can easily mistake that restlessness and anxiety for stress rather than depression.

The sign that matters is a change in how a person sleeps, eats and copes that returns with the same season each year, whether that season is winter or summer.

Doctors diagnose seasonal affective disorder by confirming a depression first, and the season only describes when it occurs. In the American Psychiatric Association's diagnostic manual, published in 2022, SAD appears as a seasonal pattern within a mood disorder and not as a separate disorder, according to StatPearls. The National Institute of Mental Health lists three criteria for the diagnosis, making it a multi-part test of an existing illness rather than a verdict on the winter blues. The test turns on timing: depressive episodes must come more often in one particular season than at other times of year. A pattern like that can only be shown across more than one year, and one rough winter, however bad, isn't yet something a doctor can confirm. Diagnosis is a conversation about past seasons more than a single test, and it helps to remember how earlier winters went.

A questionnaire can be part of the process, but it narrows the possibilities rather than naming the illness. Seasonal timing doesn't even tell a doctor which mood disorder is at work. StatPearls reports that between 15% and 25% of people with bipolar disorder have a seasonal pattern, so a low that comes every winter can belong to bipolar disorder as well as to depression. Before settling on SAD, a clinician also has to set aside other explanations, among them generalized anxiety disorder, dysthymia, adjustment disorder, bipolar disorder, and major depressive disorder. A seasonal pattern on its own is a clue for a clinician to follow, not a diagnosis.

Putting a name to the pattern matters because the depression usually comes back. Between 50% and 70% of people see it return the next winter, according to StatPearls, so a diagnosis gives a reasonable warning about the coming season. The same range also means a real share of people get through the next winter without another episode, and the pattern is not fixed for life.

A SAD diagnosis describes how someone's illness has behaved so far, and it is worth checking against each new winter rather than treating it as permanent.

The risk of seasonal affective disorder is higher in the north than in the south. People who live in places such as Alaska and New England face a higher risk, while people in places such as Texas and Florida face a lower one, according to the National Institute of Mental Health. These states mark the two ends of a broad regional difference, not a measured rate for any one place. For someone living in the north, a low mood that returns winter after winter deserves a closer look rather than a shrug about the cold.

Age also shapes when SAD tends to appear, and the disorder usually begins in young adulthood. A seasonal pattern that first shows up in the early adult years is worth raising with a doctor while it is new, before a person spends years treating the same slump as a normal part of the calendar. The typical age describes most cases, not all of them, so a first episode later in life doesn't rule SAD out.

A person with SAD can have another condition at the same time, such as an anxiety disorder, panic disorder, an eating disorder, or ADHD. Anyone already living with one of these conditions has good reason to tell a clinician about mood changes that follow the seasons. A clinician is better placed than the patient to sort out which symptoms belong to which problem.

Place, age, and other conditions show when to look harder at a seasonal low, but none of them decides who is safe from it.

Winter depression is often blamed simply on too little sun, but the leading explanation for it is more specific and involves brain chemistry. People with SAD have less serotonin, and those with the winter pattern most of all, the National Institute of Mental Health reports, and people with the winter form also make more melatonin than normal. That points to an imbalance running in two directions at once rather than a shortage of a single chemical. As their serotonin and melatonin levels change, people with SAD lose their normal daily rhythm, according to HealthDay. In that account, winter SAD is as much a disorder of the body's daily clock as of mood. The account gives the winter form a coherent explanation, but it is a theory that researchers are still working to confirm.

The explanation for the summer form reverses the winter one and carries less confidence. In summer-pattern SAD, melatonin levels may run low instead of high, and so far that idea is only a possibility. Researchers have not yet tested these theories in a systematic way, so for people whose depression arrives in warm weather, the cause remains an untested idea. Anyone with the summer form should not assume that what is understood about winter SAD applies to their own case.

Negative thoughts about the season itself may also play a part in SAD. "It is unclear, however, whether these thoughts are causes or effects of the mood disorder, but they can be a useful focus of treatment," the National Institute of Mental Health said. Clinicians can work on that pattern of thinking without first deciding whether it caused the depression or grew out of it.

Treatment does not have to wait for the science of causes to be settled.

The best-known treatment for winter SAD sounds gentle, but it is a strong dose of light taken on a strict schedule. Light therapy is the standard treatment, according to PBS NewsHour. The National Institute of Mental Health reports that the box used for it gives off about 20 times as much light as an ordinary room. A brighter bulb on the desk can't stand in for it. Patients sit in front of the box for about 30−45 minutes daily. The hour of the session and its regularity also count as part of the dose. "The research best supports a half an hour of 10,000 lux of light seven days a week before 8 a.m.," said Dr. Paul Desan, director of the Winter Depression Research Clinic at the Yale School of Medicine. Following that schedule means building an early session into nearly every winter morning, which is a real commitment for anyone who chooses light therapy.

Anyone can buy a light box without a prescription, but some people shouldn't start using one on their own. People with retinal disease or bipolar disorder face particular risks from light therapy, according to PBS NewsHour, and the treatment should begin under a medical professional's supervision. The right first step is a visit to a doctor, not a trip to the store.

People who can't keep a daily light routine still have a proven option. In a direct comparison, a form of cognitive behavioral therapy designed for SAD matched light therapy in how well it worked, according to the National Institute of Mental Health. Desan tells PBS NewsHour that the therapist questions a patient's negative beliefs and urges the patient to stay active, rather than let fatigue lead to a retreat from the activities that help. A reader can weigh whether that kind of work suits them better than a lamp at dawn.

Many people assume that the "sunshine vitamin" can fix a condition tied to dark winters, but it hasn't earned that trust. Research on whether vitamin D eases SAD has not come out consistently, so a pill can't take the place of a light box or therapy.

Take the light routine and the therapy option to a clinician, and treat a vitamin as an extra at most.

People who have had SAD can plan for their next episode before it starts, because the episodes tend to return with the same season. The National Institute of Mental Health says that someone whose past episodes came in winter might gain from beginning treatment ahead of the fall. For them, the decision comes in late summer, while they still feel well and before the days grow shorter. People whose episodes come in summer follow the reverse calendar, and their time to start treatment falls before spring. In both patterns, the signal to act is the timing of last year's episode, not how a person feels today. The practical step is to raise treatment with a clinician before the season arrives rather than in the middle of it.

Starting treatment early is a reasonable step to discuss with a doctor, but it does not guarantee that an episode won't come. Only a few studies have looked at preventing SAD, which is why the advice stays cautious. With so little research done, early treatment counts as a possible help rather than a proven defense. An extended-release form of the medication bupropion has approval from the U.S. Food and Drug Administration. A reader therefore has at least one specific medication to ask a doctor about when planning ahead, even though research on prevention as a whole remains thin. SAD is predictable enough to plan for, but only more studies can show whether that planning keeps it away.

SAD remains a condition that doctors treat with partial knowledge. Research is how medicine will answer the open questions about what causes it, how to treat its summer form and whether it can be prevented. A trial exists mainly to produce knowledge for the patients who come later, not to treat the person who enrolls. "The main purpose of a clinical trial is to gain new scientific knowledge so that others may be better helped in the future," the National Institute of Mental Health said. A person thinking about joining should weigh the decision mainly as a contribution to that knowledge.

Signing up for a study is a different decision from seeking care. A person with active winter depression needs treatment from a clinician first. Joining a trial is a separate choice that someone makes for the sake of others, ideally once their own care is in place.

People run into the unanswered questions about SAD in ordinary decisions, such as whether to buy a vitamin D supplement at the pharmacy. Research on whether vitamin D eases SAD has come back mixed, so a buyer has no firm reason to expect the pill to work. Researchers turn mixed results into firm answers only by gathering more evidence, and clinical studies with enough volunteers are how they gather it. Every question about SAD that remains open today stays open until people agree to take part in that work.

People who volunteer now give the next winter's patients what earlier volunteers gave today's: treatment advice backed by evidence rather than by guesswork.
