Brenda Song Says Suite Life Cast Was “Underpaid and Overworked”

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Brenda Song shared why it felt like she and her Suite Life of Zack & Cody costars were “underpaid and overworked” by Disney Channel, noting it felt like the cast actually paid the network.

Brenda Song’s bank account isn’t as suite as it could be.

The Suite Life of Zack & Cody star reflected on her time on Disney Channel, noting that it felt like she and her costars’ checked in for hard work but checked out with little to show for it.

As Song told her costar Dylan Sprouse on the Nov. 10 episode of his Wildmen podcast, “I look at that time and I was like, we paid them.”

And while the 38-year-old emphasized she had a “lovely time” playing hotel heiress London Tipton from 2005 to 2011 on Suite Life and its spinoff series The Suite Life on Deck, there’s one qualm that is still setting sail.

As she sees it, “The only thing that was hard about that time was I feel like we were underpaid and overworked.”

In fact, Song—mom to kids Dakota, born in 2021, and Carson, born in 2022, with fiancé Macaulay Culkin—is shocked that her time on the show hasn’t put her in the penthouse. Looking back at her years starring alongside Sprouse, his twin brother Cole Sprouse and Ashley Tisdale, the Running Point actress noted, “We basically did and the fact that we shot hundreds of episodes and our kids should be set.”

“If our show existed on any other network television channel at that time, we would have seven generations of children that would not have to worry about what they do,” the 34-year-old—dad to daughter Sylvie, 2 months, with wife Barbara Palvin—said. “The amount of income would be completely different.”

Still, Song is grateful for her time on the Disney Channel, during which she also starred in hit movies like The Ultimate Christmas Present, Get a Clue, Stuck in the Suburbs and Wendy Wu: Homecoming Warrior.

“Disney did give us a platform to be sitting here right now,” she explained. “So it’s very hard to complain about certain things.”

But, Song also added, “I look back on it and I was like oh my goodness we did actually pay them to work.”

E! News has reached out to Disney for comment but has not yet heard back.

Song is not the only star who has candidly detailed their paychecks. For more, keep reading…

The Jackass star reflected on the "comical" amount of money he was paid for doing death-defying stunts on the MTV series' first season in 2000.

"When it was all said and done, after taxes, I was paid less than $1,500 for the entire first season of Jackass," the daredevil told Playboy in June 2026. "Before the show came out, my sister kicked me out of the house. I was broke, unemployed, and homeless, and a star on this big MTV show."

Oscar winner Jennifer Lawrence was paid $25 million to star in Adam McKay’s ensemble disaster comedy Don’t Look Up. But her costar and fellow Oscar winner Leonardo DiCaprio got $30 million.

“Look, Leo brings in more box office than I do,” Lawrence told Vanity Fair for its’ December 2020/January 2021 issue. “I’m extremely fortunate and happy with my deal. But in other situations, what I have seen—and I’m sure other women in the workforce have seen as well—is that it’s extremely uncomfortable to inquire about equal pay. And if you do question something that appears unequal, you’re told it’s not gender disparity but they can’t tell you what exactly it is.”

Her sanguine attitude was hard-fought: When the disparity between Lawrence’s compensation and that of her male costars for 2013’s American Hustle was revealed in the 2014 Sony email hack, it prompted an industry-wide conversation about the gender pay gap in Hollywood.

"I'm so fortunate to have my job. My problem is not money," Lawrence told the U.K.’s Channel 4 News in 2017. "I wasn't upset that I only got this many millions for a movie. That's ridiculous. I was angry about the unfairness and inequality."

The *NSYNC alum revealed that he and his fellow bandmates JC Chasez, Joey Fatone, Chris Kirkpatrick and Justin Timberlake didn't make much money during their years with the group.

“We had a per diem: $35 a day,” he revealed on The Zach Sang Show in 2026. “Our first check was a $10,000 check after years of No. 1 albums, sellout tours. I don’t know how many millions they made off of merchandise because merchandise was insane.”

“And then they finally gave us a check for $10,000,” he recalled. “You’re like, ‘Oh, thanks.’ We should be grateful for that, been working for free for two, three years now.”

Less comparable to DiCaprio’s salary: The $60,000 “before commissions and taxes” that Jonah Hill was paid for 2013’s The Wolf of Wall Street, while the titular wolf made $10 million.

“They gave me the lowest amount of money possible,” Hill recalled on The Howard Stern Show in 2014. But it was the chance to appear in a Martin Scorsese movie, so the Moneyball alum wanted to get the deal done before anyone could change their mind.

“I would sell my house and give him all my money to work for [Scorsese],” explained Hill, who earned his second Best Supporting Actor nomination for his turn as a squirrelly substance-abusing stockbroker. “This isn't what you make money for. You do 22 Jump Street or you do other things, and you can pay your rent. I would have done anything in the world. I would do it again in a second. This isn't about money. You should do things that you care about."

According to Dylan Sprouse, his residuals from filming The Suite Life of Zack & Cody with twin brother Cole Sprouse is anything but suite.

"The only residual check I've ever received for The Suite Life at all was a crossover episode with Hannah Montana for five cents," the Disney Channel alum shared during a 2026 episode of his Wildmen podcast. "In a time where residuals would have been great, not a single paycheck for being arguably one of the most popular shows on television."

While there's no cash prize for nabbing Dancing With the Stars' mirrorball trophy, “whoever makes it to the finale, you all get paid up until then," Jenna Johnson, who won season 33 with partner Joey Graziadei, shared. "And you do get a nice bonus.”

"If you win," Johnson, who noted pro contracts are dependent on seniority, continued. "it’s not like you get a million dollars. You’re just getting a cute trophy together.” As for those who say farewell to the ballroom early, well, as she explained, "You’re guaranteed until a certain amount of weeks.” According to multiple reports, dancer salaries start at around $1,200 to $1,600 an episode, which can increase to up to of $100,000 per season. (ABC has not confirmed these reports.) On the flip side, celebrities receive a starting salary of $125,000. As they cha-cha further into the competition, stars  receive additional bonuses with the semifinalists and finalists getting paid up to $50,000 extra.

The actress seemingly confirmed that the Friends cast—including Jennifer Aniston, Courtney Cox,David Schwimmer and Matt LeBlanc—get $20 million in residuals per year from the show re-airing, quipping to The Times in April 2026 that if she had to guess why, it's “Because Phoebe Buffay was so great?"

But in actuality, she praised the talents of her costars for their performances.

“I felt I did OK, but Jennifer and Courteney? Amazing,” she raved. “David and Matt? They had me laughing so hard. And then Matthew [Perry]—he was just beyond us all.”

To hear Joe Giudice tell it, his and ex-wife Teresa Giudice's salary for season one of The Real Housewives of New Jersey was nothing to flip over. "We got paid nothing the first season," he detailed on daughter Gia Giudice's Casual Chaos podcast in August 2025. "I'll even say, it was like $25,000. It was ridiculous. But, whatever."

And the ingredientses they put into that initial fame-making season weren't cheap. "I think the first year we had that big party in my great room," the father of four reflected. "I think that show cost me $150,000 the first year."

Oprah Winfrey happily accepted only a little bit of green to star in 1985’s The Color Purple.

“They were only offering $35,000 to be in this film, and it is the best $35,000 I ever earned,” the billionaire media mogul told Essence in 2023. “It changed everything and taught me so much.”

The actress revealed that she made about $20,000 per episode of Two and a Half Men with a 5 percent raise each season, making about $43,000 for one episode in the final season.

When she scored the role of late Tejana singer Selena Quintanilla in the 1997 biopic Selena, Jennifer Lopez became the first Latina actress to earn $1 million for a film.

“I was too young and didn’t know what the hell was going on,” Lopez told Variety in 2019, reflecting on her breakout movie. “It was great they offered me a million dollars. I feel like everyone was making a statement.”

And it paved the way for her to make hundreds of millions more, though sometimes her participation doesn’t cost a thing.

“I do things because I love them,” Lopez explained to GQ in 2019. “I didn't get paid a whole bunch of money for Hustlers. I did it for free and produced it. I bank on myself, you know? Like Jenny From the Block—I do what I love."

Adrien Brody didn’t do The Brutalist for the money. Rather, he took on the role of a Hungarian architect suffering for his art in post-WWII America for $250,000 and won his second Oscar for Best Actor.

“I need a studio movie now, because I’ve poured it all into this,” Brody quipped to Variety, referring to the “barn that looks like a castle” he’s renovating in upstate New York.

“Do you guys know what they pay us?” Pete Davidson cracked to New York magazine when asked ahead of Saturday Night Live's 50th anniversary special how he spent his money when he was first hired at the age of 20 in 2014. “It’s like three grand an episode, so I think I got dinner.”

Interestingly, the salaries have remained proportional for SNL newcomers, with season one stars John Belushi, Jane Curtin, Gilda Radner, Dan Aykroyd and Chevy Chase reportedly earning $750 an episode back in 1975. Which was roughly equivalent to $3,300 in 2014, according to the Consumer Price Index Inflation calculator. (Though the $750 of 1975 is $4,428 in 2025.)

While Kenan Thompson makes a lot more than $3,000 per episode of SNL now that he’s in his record 22nd season, the former Nickelodeon star had to start somewhere.

“That first commercial when they paid me, it was $800,” the All That alum revealed in Demi Lovato‘s 2024 documentary Child Star. “I was 12 so that may as well have been a million dollars."

It turns out Gilmore Girls’ status as a beloved rewatchable classic is literally priceless.

“There really are no residuals on Netflix,” Lauren Graham, who played Lorelai Gilmore for seven seasons and then reprised the role for 2016’s Gilmore Girls: A Year in the Life, said on Jimmy Kimmel Live in March 2025. “But I’ve been paid in love and appreciation.”

Feeling not so appreciated, Tommy Dorfman got specific about her 13 Reasons Why paycheck during the 2023 SAG-AFTRA strike—which was prompted in part (as was the WGA strike) by streamers like Netflix not making it a practice to pay additional compensation to cast and crew once a show had become a big hit.

"my earnings for the entire first season of 13 reasons why were $29,953.24 prior to agency and manager fees (20%) and taxes," Dorfman wrote on Threads July 24. "8 episodes over six months." She continued, “i did all of the promo and had KEY ART for this show, flew round trip from NYC to SF to shoot for every episode, was kept for days without pay/working. i barely qualified for insurance."

And, Dorfman added, “within the first 28 days of release, the show's season 1 garnered a total of 476 million view hours. this is why we strike."

Without naming names or numbers, Dax Shepard told fellow Parenthood alum Lauren Graham when she appeared on his podcast that he knew he made “among the lowest of all the actors” on their NBC drama, which ran for six seasons.

But, the Armchair Expert host stressed that he did not mind.

“Every job I had ever had in my life prior to Parenthood, I made a point to find out what everyone was making,” he said. “I'd always figure it out—either a conversation where I get it out of them or I backchannel through an agent."

So he made a point to purposely not seek out such info about his Parenthood family, which helped with enjoying the experience even when he did get an inkling of where he was on the pay pyramid.

“I got a dollar for writing the script,” George Clooney told the Los Angeles Times in 2005 about the financial strings he pulled to get his film Good Night, and Good Luck made. “I had to endorse my check for directing and turn in my acting salary. [Actor, cowriter and coproducer Grant Heslov] and I each made a buck for doing it.”

His labor of love about CBS News journalist Edward R. Murrow speaking truth to power in the 1950s had legs, though: Making his Broadway debut, Clooney wrapped his months-long stint playing Murrow in a stage adaptation of the film  in June 2025. And with the play having grossed a Broadway record (for a nonmusical) $3.3 million during a preview week before it officially opened April 3, he made a lot more than $1.

For his chilling turn as Patrick Bateman in 2000’s American Psycho, Christian Bale was paid “the absolute minimum they were legally allowed to pay me” because no one in the production really wanted to hire him except director Mary Harron, the Welsh actor told GQ in 2022.

He made so little, Bale shared, that he remembered “sitting in the makeup trailer and the makeup artists were laughing at me because I was getting paid less than any of them.”

No, the Game of Thrones cast didn't get paid $300,000 per episode, according to Emilia Clarke.

“We didn’t earn that much," she told Variety in 2026. "Can you imagine? I’d have been driving a couple of Porsches!"

However, the actress—who played Daenerys Targaryen for eight seasons between 2011 and 2019—noted that the money she made throughout the HBO show did provide her with enough financial security to pay off her parents’ mortgage.

His uncle may have been Rico, but Jon Heder confirmed in 2010 that he was “initially” only paid $1,000 for Napoleon Dynamite.

After the irreverent comedy became a sleeper hit, he was able to renegotiate for a piece of the profits, telling the New Zealand Herald, “They went a little bit higher.”

Djimon Hounsou said he’s “definitely underpaid” in Hollywood despite his prestigious list of credits, including Amistad and Blood Diamond.

"I've been in this business and making films now over two decades," the Beninese actor told CNN’s Larry Madowo in January 2025. "And still—with two Oscar nominations and been in many big blockbuster films—and yet I’m still struggling financially to make a living."

Rebel Wilson detailed in her 2024 memoir Rebel Rising that she was paid $3,500 to do Bridesmaids, “a fee that I then had to pay directly to the Screen Actors Guild to join the union. So really, I got paid nothing.”

Still, she wrote, the experience was “everything!”

And the Australian actress’ star rose ever a-ca higher, with her fee going from the SAG minimum scale of $65,000 for 2012’s Pitch Perfect to $10 million for 2017’s Pitch Perfect 3. Wilson wrote that she raked in $20 million total for the threequel, Isn’t It Romantic and The Hustle, though—reality alert—she “lost almost 50 percent to taxes, 10 percent to agents and 5 percent” to her lawyer. But even after those obligations and paying her publicist, business manager and assistant, Wilson “netted what to me was an absolute fortune.”

In late 2017, Ellen Pompeo negotiated a new Grey’s Anatomy contract for $575,000 per episode, a seven-figure signing bonus and two full backend equity points worth an estimated $6 million to $7 million.

“I’m 48 now, so I’ve finally gotten to the place where I’m OK asking for what I deserve, which is something that comes only with age,” Pompeo told The Hollywood Reporter in 2018. “Because I’m not the most ‘relevant’ actress out there. I know that’s the industry perception because I’ve been this character for 14 years. But the truth is, anybody can be good on a show season one and two. Can you be good 14 years later? Now, that’s a f--kin’ skill.”

Reflecting more recently on how she always made less than costar Patrick Dempsey, even though she was the Grey in question, Pompeo said she didn’t begrudge him his money.

But, she said on a March 2025 episode of Call Her Daddy, “Just being that I was the namesake of the show, I deserved the same and that was harder to get. I wasn't salty about him getting what he got. I was salty that they didn't value me as much as they valued him and they never will.”

Jamie Lee Curtis recalled getting paid $2,000 a week—so $8,000 in total—for 1978’s Halloween, her movie debut. “I mean,” she told Rotten Tomatoes in 2018, “nobody got paid, anything.”

Yet while the scream queen earned four figures, director John Carpenter’s friend Nick Castle felt like a king making $25 per day to share the role of Michael Myers. (It’s Anthony Moran whose face you see when the killer’s mask comes off for a freaky second, and editor Tommy Lee Wallace also logged screentime as Michael.)

“That was a lot at the time!” Castle, who attended film school at USC with Carpenter, told Vanity Fair in 2018. “You have to remember: My interest in doing the film was being on set, so I could demystify the experience of filmmaking and directing. I expected to hang around the set for no money. But hey, $25 per day, and all I had to do was wear a rubber mask.”

Taraji P. Henson earned a Best Supporting Actress Oscar nomination for 2008’s The Curious Case of Benjamin Button, but years later she was still fired up by the $150,000 she accepted to be in the movie—after producers rejected the $500,000 quote she brought to the table.

As for the film’s leads Brad Pitt and Cate Blanchett, “You don’t hear a lot of $20 million quotes anymore, but at this time that was happening,” Henson said on the Ladies First With Laura Brown podcast in 2021. “And rightfully so—I’m not saying they shouldn’t have paid Cate and Brad what they deserved.”

But, she continued, “I’m bringing a certain amount to the seat too and I felt like what I was asking at that time in my career was fair, was fair to the ticket sales that I would contribute to this big film. Wouldn’t do it.” Henson was “gutted” when they offered $100,000, she recalled. “When it was all said and done I got $150,000, but I had to swallow my pride, baby.”

Breaking it down, “I know people go, ‘$150,000, that’s a lot of money!’” Henson acknowledged. “I don’t ever want people to think that I’m ungrateful because that is not me.” But, she calculated, “Uncle Sam is going to take 50 percent of that, so now you’re left with, what? $75,000. Now before Uncle Sam takes the money, I have to pay my team before taxes, 30 percent. So once Uncle Sam takes his 75, then I got another 30 that’s coming off of that 75, so I may have made $40,000?”

While Blanchett has made plenty of money over the years, it didn’t all come from playing royal elf Galadriel in The Lord of the Rings franchise, though it grossed $2.9 billion at the worldwide box office.

“Are you kidding me? No,” she retorted on Watch What Happens Live in August 2024 when Andy Cohen guessed LOTR was her biggest paycheck. “No one got paid anything to do that movie…I mean, I basically got free sandwiches, and I got to keep my ears.”

They weren’t paid a precious amount, that is. Orlando Bloom, who played elven warrior Legolas, shared on The Howard Stern Show in 2023 that he made $175,000 for the whole trilogy.

Thanksgiving’s 7 record-breaking TV moments, and what really drove each one

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Thanksgiving 2025 set NFL, streaming and parade viewing records. See which gains reflect real demand and which reflect Nielsen's new counting.

A growing share of American TV time goes to viewing on each person's own schedule, with people picking shows from apps and libraries instead of tuning in when a network airs them. As that habit spreads, a broadcast that brings tens of millions of people to the same screen at the same moment has become rare. Thanksgiving is the exception, and its audience is getting bigger. Streaming took 46.7% of all TV watch-time in November 2025, according to Nielsen. Even so, the holiday's football audience set a record for the fourth straight year, according to The Associated Press. Networks and the NFL prize the day because viewers keep gathering for it while they drift apart for nearly everything else.

The more people split up across screens, the more a live event they all watch together is worth to the companies that sell it. "What really drives this business is the unique ability of sports to bring people together and create shared experiences in real time, and those shared experiences become more rare and more valuable when there's so much media content out there that is driving people into more isolated experiences," said Mike Mulvihill, president of insights and analytics at Fox Sports. This year's numbers come with a caveat, though. The Associated Press reports that Nielsen switched to a new way of measuring audiences for every event starting Sept. 1, 2025. NFL Commissioner Roger Goodell says the holiday audience probably came in above the league's projections and calls it helpful that the league can now count viewers who went uncounted in the past, according to The Associated Press. For every record, the real question is how much came from more people watching and how much came from better counting.

Axios looked at which 2025 Thanksgiving broadcasts drew record audiences and what drove them, from the Cowboys-Chiefs game and the holiday's night game to streaming and the Macy's $M Thanksgiving Day Parade. The records don't share a single cause. Some depend on a marquee matchup, some on the ways people now reach a broadcast, and one has nothing to do with football at all. The counting question matters to the league as much as to anyone, since in September the NFL had accused Nielsen of undercounting its viewers. What follows weighs each record against the other explanations for it, so readers can tell a lasting appetite from a one-year bump.

Thanksgiving's football audience set a record this year by a margin wide enough to raise questions about what drove it. The holiday's three NFL games averaged nearly 45 million viewers, according to Axios, and they beat last year's record of 34.5 million by a wide margin. A jump that large in a single year needs an explanation before anyone reads it as a surge of new interest. Two explanations besides more fans also fit this number: the way the audience was counted and the games the league placed on the holiday.

The Associated Press reports that Nielsen's count now includes what people watch on smart TVs. The gap between the two records likely combines people who started watching with people who were already watching but weren't counted before. No available figure separates those two groups. The record holds under this season's count, but the size of the jump likely overstates how much interest actually grew.

The matchups on the holiday also shaped the number, and networks compete for a limited supply of the strongest games. Mike Mulvihill, Fox Sports president, insight and analytics, told The Associated Press that "at some level it is a little bit zero sum." Fox has next year's Dallas Thanksgiving game, so next year's audience will depend on a different network's choices and a different set of matchups. A repeat of this year's average would take more than steady demand.

The record proves that Americans still gather in front of one broadcast on Thanksgiving. Whether more of them gather than a year ago is still an open question.

The NFL's regular-season audience record has become a Dallas Thanksgiving tradition. The Cowboys' holiday game against the Kansas City Chiefs had 57.2 million viewers on CBS, the biggest regular-season audience in NFL history, according to Axios. NFL.com reports that the previous record came from the Giants-Cowboys game on Thanksgiving 2022, and this year's audience was 36% larger. A gap that wide is too large to put down to ordinary change from one year to the next. The credit belongs first to the date and the team. Much of the country already spends Thanksgiving afternoon watching the Cowboys, and a record in that slot says more about that habit than about a sudden new appetite for football.

This year's matchup added stakes that the holiday slot doesn't guarantee. "It so happens they were both coming off last-second, come-from-behind victories and they're fighting for their playoff lives," David Berson, CBS Sports president and CEO, told The Associated Press. Those stakes grew out of how both teams' seasons unfolded in the weeks before the holiday. The Cowboys won 31-28, according to NFL.com, in a game that stayed in doubt to the end. The audience reached its peak of 61.3 million in the final moments, well above the average for the whole broadcast. Nobody can schedule a three-point finish, or two teams with their playoff hopes at risk on the same afternoon.

The record shows what the holiday can do when everything lines up, not what it will do every year.

Thanksgiving afternoon fills living rooms even when the Dallas Cowboys aren't playing. Some 47.7 million people watched the Green Bay Packers and the Detroit Lions on Fox, according to Axios. That crowd beat Fox's regular-season record of 42.06 million, set by a Giants-Cowboys game in 2022, according to The Associated Press. For a short time, the early game also held the record for the biggest regular-season audience in NFL history, until the later Cowboys-Chiefs game on CBS topped it. The holiday's largest audience grew so much in one year that a record set in the first game fell before the day's football was over. The fair reading is that the whole afternoon drew a deeper audience this year, not that Green Bay and Detroit held some special appeal.

The average also understates how many people watched at the same moment. At the game's busiest stretch, 57.96 million viewers were tuned in, according to The Associated Press. That peak is the truer measure of how many households the early game gathered at once, and a crowd of that size is what makes the holiday so valuable to the networks that sell it.

The Fox audience is not only people watching Fox. Awful Announcing reports that the total combines viewers on Fox and on Tubi, and that the early game's audience rose 28% from the same game last year. Nielsen changed how it counts this season, though, so this year can't be lined up cleanly against last year. Some of the gain is likely real appetite, given how far the game passed the old Giants-Cowboys mark. Some of it comes from wider counting, and nobody can yet say how much of each.

The record also rested on a choice Fox made. "I think it was entirely strategically correct to really go for it this year and try to get the biggest number," Mike Mulvihill, Fox Sports president, insight and analytics, told The Associated Press. He says Fox will have to weigh how aggressive to be next year, when the network has the Dallas Thanksgiving game. The executive in charge of Fox's analytics won't promise the same all-out push, so the 2025 number came from a deliberate bet as much as from demand.

Next year's early game will show whether the appetite lasts without Fox pushing as hard for the biggest number.

The Thanksgiving night game drew the smallest crowd of the holiday's three games, and it was still a huge audience. It came at the end of a long day, and it matched the Cincinnati Bengals and the Baltimore Ravens rather than the holiday's best-known teams. Even so, 28.4 million people watched, according to Axios, and few broadcasts ever see a crowd that size. That total combines NBC, Peacock and Telemundo, Awful Announcing reports, so it includes English-language TV, Spanish-language TV and streaming in one count. Fans followed the holiday's last game on whatever screen they use, and together they still formed a mass audience.

This number also includes some viewers who were simply counted for the first time. Nielsen's new way of measuring live sports is estimated to add 5-15% to audiences, according to Awful Announcing, and part of the 28.4 million likely watched in past years without showing up in the count. That cuts both ways. NBC did not need a marquee pairing to post a big figure, but nobody can read that figure as proof that interest in the holiday is growing.

With no afternoon slot and no headline teams, the night game is the closest measure of the day's own appeal. That measure supports a modest claim. Tens of millions of Americans watch football on Thanksgiving night even without the league's biggest names on the field, so the holiday's audience has a high floor. This year's figure cannot separate a lasting rise from a counting bump, though.

A fairer test of whether the Thanksgiving night habit is growing will come next year, when both night games are counted the same way.

Thanksgiving viewers turned to apps to watch football in far larger numbers this year. The holiday games averaged 2.2 million streaming viewers, a 58% jump from last year and streaming's best Thanksgiving yet, according to NFL figures reported by Axios. A rise that steep in one year reflects a sudden shift in how people watched rather than a slow drift onto apps. At first glance, that shift suggests the holiday audience is leaving traditional TV for streaming services.

On the same holiday, people also used apps in large numbers to watch live TV channels, the same kind of broadcast that streaming is supposed to replace. Nielsen reports that viewing live channels through pay-TV apps reached its second-highest daily level ever on Thanksgiving and made up 10.1% of all TV use, well above its share across November. Only on Super Bowl Sunday in February 2025 did more people watch live channels this way. Nielsen tracks this channel viewing separately from streaming services, so the two figures measure different habits. Both, however, point to people on Thanksgiving using apps to reach TV that was airing live.

The rest of the day's viewing suggests that people may be adding apps as another way to watch broadcast TV rather than abandoning it. If so, the streaming record is partly a record about access, with interest in the holiday's games reaching viewers through a different kind of service. On that reading, the 58% jump says less about new viewers than it first appears and more about broadcasters' ability to reach their audience on whatever screen it uses.

The holiday's crowd didn't leave for streaming. Streaming became one more way that crowd gathers.

Thanksgiving's records came in a season when NFL audiences are larger across the board, not only on the holiday. More than 17 million people watched the average NFL game through week 12, according to Axios, which means an ordinary regular-season game now draws an audience the size of a major TV event. That average is also climbing, with per-game viewership up 6% from last year. Part of the holiday's gain is the same rise in football viewing that fans brought to every other week of the season. Thanksgiving set its records in a year when more people were watching NFL games on ordinary Sundays, too.

The season's increase is no cleaner a measure of fan demand than the holiday records are. The 6% gain sets this year's games against last year's, and Nielsen changed how it measures audiences in between. Nielsen's new method adds data from smart TVs to its traditional representative panel of viewers, so the company now counts viewing on sets that report what they play. People the panel alone would have missed can therefore show up this year as growth. None of this takes away from what Thanksgiving accomplished. It means the same caution applies to the whole year: on these numbers, nobody can yet say how much of the holiday's gain or the season's gain comes from more people watching.

Only comparisons counted the same way in both years will show whether fans found a new appetite for football or Nielsen found a new way to count them.

Americans gather around one broadcast on Thanksgiving even when there is no game to watch. Viewership for the Macy's $M Thanksgiving Day Parade grew 8% from last year, according to Axios, and its audience of 34.3 million was a record. Those viewers watched balloons and floats, with no score to follow and no final whistle to wait for. This year's gain was not the parade's first recent increase, since its audience also rose 6% in 2023, according to CNBC. No team, rivalry or pairing of opponents was part of the broadcast, so the parade's record cannot be credited to a matchup the way a football record can.

This year's record can't settle whether broadcast TV is holding its ground, because the 34.3 million total combines NBC viewers with Peacock streams. In 2023, about 22.3 million people watched the parade on traditional TV alone, according to Variety figures reported by CNBC. The gain since then can't be credited to broadcast on its own. That 2023 audience made the parade a strong argument for old-fashioned television. "When it gets the right content in the right context, it blows everything else away," Patrick Crakes, a sports media consultant and former Fox Sports executive, said of traditional TV at the time. This year's blended total can neither confirm nor refute his case.

Nobody can yet say whether Nielsen's new counting method, which applies to the NFL games, also changed the parade's figure. The parade's rise can't be traced in any part to that change, and it can't be cleared of it either.

People clearly still gather together on Thanksgiving morning, but this year's record can't show whether they do it on broadcast TV or on a streaming app.

FKA Twigs Becomes Josephine Baker in First Look at Maïmouna Doucouré’s Buzzy Biopic

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Grammy-winning singer stars in the ‘Cuties’ director’s portrait of the Jazz Age icon and civil rights activist.

StudioCanal and Bien ou Bien Productions have unveiled the first official image of FKA Twigs as Josephine Baker in Maïmouna Doucouré’s buzzy upcoming biopic, giving buyers at the American Film Market an early glimpse of the star’s uncanny transformation into the Jazz Age icon. (See the image below)

Released Wednesday, the still shows Twigs striking one of Baker’s classic playful poses atop a dining table in a pink, beaded and fringed dress, her hair styled in the singer’s signature close-cropped waves.

Twigs’ flair for theatrical reinvention makes her an inspired fit for Baker, whose performances fused sensual glamour with a mischievous physical wit. The image captures some of that combination, with Twigs flashing a broad smile as she twists toward her audience. Related Stories Movies Suzanne Farwell Bets on a Big-Screen Comeback for the Rom-Com With 'Otherwise Engaged' Movies 'Tangled' Takes Ciudad de la Luz From Up-and-Coming Studio to Production Must-Stop

Written by Doucouré (Cuties, Hawa) with Olivier Lorelle, Josephine Baker will follow its subject’s rise to international stardom in 1920s Paris and her subsequent work with the French Resistance and the American civil rights movement.

Born in St. Louis in 1906, Baker moved to Paris in 1925 and became one of the defining entertainers of the Jazz Age. Her later activism included appearing alongside Martin Luther King Jr. at the 1963 March on Washington. In 2021, she became the first Black woman inducted into France’s Panthéon.

“I cannot wait to embody Josephine Baker, bringing her fight, her love, her losses, her talent and her heroism to the big screen,” Twigs said when her casting was announced in May. “She lives on in our hearts as a visionary, ground-breaking woman whose story is as powerful as it is relevant today.”

The project has the support of Baker’s sons Jean-Claude Bouillon Baker and Brian Bouillon Baker, along with the Rainbow Tribe, the multiracial family of 12 children she raised at her château in southwest France.

Twigs won her first Grammy in February, taking best dance/electronic album for Eusexua. Her prior screen credits include Alma Har’el’s Honey Boy, Rupert Sanders’ The Crow and David Lowery’s Mother Mary, opposite Anne Hathaway and Michaela Coel.

StudioCanal and Bien ou Bien Productions are producing, with CPB Films, Leyland Films and Dingie as associated producers. StudioCanal is handling worldwide sales at AFM and will release the film theatrically in France, the U.K., Germany, Italy, Benelux, Poland, Australia and New Zealand.

10 things to know about RSV in babies, from symptoms to prevention

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The CDC's guide to RSV in babies covers early symptoms, who faces the most risk, when to seek emergency care and the two ways to protect infants.

Most parents have sat up with a baby through a cold, and RSV in an infant often looks like nothing more than that. The stakes are far higher. "RSV was the leading cause of infant hospitalization in the U.S. before the introduction of RSV immunizations," the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention said. A family also has less to fall back on once a baby is sick. Widely available antiviral treatments exist for flu and COVID-19, according to the CDC, and RSV is not among them. That makes RSV a serious illness that families do better to head off than to wait out. The useful work happens before a baby gets sick and in the first watchful days of illness, not at the pharmacy counter.

Prevention has become possible, because babies can now be protected before they catch the virus. Most babies still begin the season without that protection. Only 39.6% of U.S. infants were protected by a maternal vaccine or an infant antibody in October 2025, as the season began, according to the CDC. Many families therefore have to rely on spotting trouble early. That is hard when the first signs of RSV look like those of an ordinary cold, and when a baby's age matters more than the baby's health history. Parents of a healthy, full-term newborn have no reason to assume their child is safe from serious illness.

The CDC explained how RSV affects babies and how families can shield them, so parents could understand both what the illness does and how to prevent it. Its guidance covers the quieter signs that show up in the youngest infants, the moments that call for emergency care, and the choice between a vaccine during pregnancy and an antibody for the baby. Many of a family's biggest decisions about RSV come before birth and in a baby's first weeks, when parents and doctors can time protection to the season. Those decisions work best when parents plan them ahead with a doctor. Protection lowers a baby's risk without erasing it, though. Knowing the warning signs still matters for every baby, protected or not.

For small children, a hospital stay for RSV was never a rare event. The CDC estimates that each year in the U.S., before immunizations became available, RSV put 58,000 to 80,000 children under 5 in the hospital. With tens of thousands of young children admitted each year, a family whose child caught the virus faced a real chance of a hospital stay, even though most infections are mild. That outcome is the one parents are working to prevent.

Immunizing babies can reduce that toll without ending it. The CDC projects that maternal vaccines and infant antibody shots together could prevent about 48% of RSV hospitalizations in babies under 1 if uptake matched last season's level. That would be a meaningful cut from the tens of thousands of yearly admissions before immunizations. Many babies would still end up in the hospital, though, and families have good reason to plan around that risk.

The danger is also building this winter. In its December update, the CDC reports that emergency visits and hospitalizations for RSV are rising among children up to age 4. A rising season is not necessarily a worse one. The agency expects this season's national peak in hospitalization rates to be similar to last season's, but only with moderate confidence. That forecast covers people of all ages rather than infants alone. For babies, the size of the benefit depends on how many of them have protection, so the outcome will turn partly on how many families act before the peak.

Hospital visits are climbing now, and families still have time to protect a baby before the season peaks.

Careful households can keep a baby away from coughing relatives and crowded rooms, but they can rarely keep RSV out of a young child's life for good. Most children have had RSV by age 2, according to the CDC. For a baby, then, catching the virus in the first couple of years is a normal part of early childhood, not a stroke of bad luck that only a few families suffer. RSV is common everywhere, with an estimated 64 million cases worldwide each year, according to NIAID. No family can count on its habits or its address to keep a child clear of the virus, and parents should expect their baby to catch RSV sooner or later.

With most children catching RSV, the question parents can act on is how well protected their baby will be at the time of infection, not whether infection will happen. The answer to that question shapes where a family should put its effort. Keeping a newborn home and asking visitors to wash their hands still helps, but those steps can only put off contact with the virus. The bigger difference comes from getting protection in place before the baby is exposed, so that the baby already has defenses when the infection arrives.

Most children catch RSV, but only some become seriously ill. That makes the next question for parents which babies face the greatest danger.

Some babies face RSV with weaker defenses than others because of how they were born or what their bodies already have to manage. Beyond young age itself, the CDC identifies a set of health conditions that make a child more likely to become seriously ill from the virus. In its guidance for doctors, the CDC lists premature infants among the children at increased risk, so being born early carries the same warning for parents and for physicians. What these babies have in common is a body that protects the airway poorly. That can come from weak lungs, a weakened immune system or a condition present from birth that makes it hard to swallow or clear mucus. For a parent, that shared weakness matters more than the name of any diagnosis. A baby who already works harder to breathe has less room to get through a respiratory infection, so even a mild start to the illness deserves closer watching.

A listed condition does not set a baby apart from other infants. Instead, it adds to a risk that every young baby already carries. The CDC's guidance for doctors puts the share of babies younger than 6 months admitted to the hospital with RSV in a given year at 2 to 3 percent, a wider age range than the agency uses for that same share in its advice to parents. The agency attaches no comparable figure to any single high-risk group. Young age is therefore the base risk that can be measured, and a condition on the list adds to it rather than replacing it. The babies with the least room for error are those who carry both risks, such as a premature or medically fragile baby in the first months of life, when the risk from age alone is already at its highest. These families gain the most from protection timed before the season and from quick action when warning signs appear.

RSV in a baby can start with a runny nose, a sign parents see all winter and rarely worry about, according to the CDC. That makes RSV easy to mistake for an ordinary cold at first. A familiar start is not a safe one, and a baby who seems only sniffly at first can be much sicker days later. In the youngest infants, a cold-like start is a reason to keep watching, and the most useful thing a parent can do is notice how those signs change.

Watching a baby for RSV takes days, and a single check is not enough. After a baby catches the virus, symptoms can take 2 to 8 days to appear, according to StatPearls. For parents, that means the days after a possible exposure call for close attention, and so do the first days of illness. A family that knows its baby was near someone sick should treat the quiet days that follow as a time to watch closely, not as proof that the baby escaped.

The signal to act is a baby who gets worse, not simply a baby who is sick. Worsening symptoms are a reason to seek emergency care, so parents should watch the direction of the illness more closely than how it started. Parents who notice that change early can get their baby to care before breathing or feeding trouble takes hold.

With RSV, a calm first day proves nothing, and the days that follow show how sick the baby is.

In the first months of life, a baby with RSV may never get the stuffy nose and cough that parents expect from a respiratory virus. Babies younger than 6 months can show signs of their own, according to the CDC, so parents can't judge a newborn by the rules that fit a toddler. One of those signs is irritability, and a parent can easily put a fussier-than-usual baby down to an ordinary bad day. Another sign runs the opposite way. A baby who becomes less active is also showing a symptom, which means a sleepier, stiller infant is not necessarily getting better. Both are changes in behavior rather than in breathing, and parents can miss them at the age when missing them matters most.

The most serious sign at this age makes no sound at all: the baby stops breathing for a stretch. A pause longer than 10 seconds counts as apnea, long enough for a watching parent to notice and too long to dismiss as a skipped breath. A parent who is listening for a cough can miss it entirely, so looking at how a baby this young breathes is part of the job. Apnea is still only one sign among several, and a baby who breathes steadily but is fussy or listless still needs attention. Together, these signs ask parents to read the whole baby.

Spotting an illness that looks like a mood or a nap is a heavy load for tired parents of a newborn. For the youngest babies, protection in place before the virus arrives may matter more than watching for symptoms, and the prevention schedule is built around this age group.

For a baby under 6 months, the safest moment to act is before any of these signs appear.

Babies and adults respond to RSV in very different ways, and babies get the worse end of it. The CDC reports that infants almost always have symptoms when infected, while adults may carry the virus without any. A baby who catches RSV will nearly always show signs of it, even when those signs stay mild. For a parent, then, the real question is whether anyone recognizes those signs for what they are.

In babies under 6 months, the first clue may be a change in mood or energy rather than a sniffle. Irritability is a symptom at this age, and a baby who is unusually fussy may be sick rather than tired or hungry. Decreased activity counts too, even though a quieter, stiller baby can look like a baby having a calm day. A normal thermometer reading doesn't settle the question either, because a baby can have RSV without a fever. The useful benchmark is the baby's own normal, and any marked shift in behavior counts, whichever direction it goes.

In the youngest babies, RSV can interrupt breathing itself. Pauses in breathing that last more than 10 seconds are a recognized symptom in infants under 6 months, and a parent watching a sleeping baby can notice and count them. This sign looks nothing like a cold. A very young infant can be sick with RSV while showing none of the symptoms parents expect.

Caring for a sick baby also means dropping adult habits at the medicine cabinet. Aspirin should never be given to children, according to the CDC, even though many adults reach for it during their own illnesses.

Spotting RSV in a baby and comforting one both mean setting aside what parents know from their own colds.

Severe RSV in a baby is the same sniffly cold, only deeper in the body than parents expect. A mild RSV cold in an infant can become bronchiolitis or pneumonia, both illnesses of the lungs, according to the CDC. The early, cold-like days are therefore no proof that a baby is in the clear. In the youngest babies, the severe form is common enough for families to plan around: among infants younger than 3 months, 2 to 3 in every 100 are hospitalized with RSV. For a newborn, a hospital stay is a realistic outcome rather than a freak one. A cold in the first months of life calls for closer watching than the same cold in an older child. Parents are not waiting for a new illness to appear. They are watching for signs that the familiar one now involves the lungs.

The youngest babies have been landing in the hospital with RSV less often since protection for infants arrived, though no one has shown how much of the drop the shots explain. Hospitalization rates among babies up to 2 months old were 52% lower than in the seasons before the pandemic in one national tracking system and 45% lower in another, according to CDC. The newborns most likely to need a hospital bed are the group where those drops appeared. The timing matches the arrival of protection, but a match in timing is not proof that the shots caused the decline. Those results also describe all babies of that age across a season and promise nothing for any single child. Even so, they are an encouraging early sign for the infants at greatest risk.

Fewer newborns may need a hospital bed for RSV in the seasons ahead, but some still will, so parents should know what happens once a baby is admitted.

A hospital stay for RSV is about helping the baby get through the illness, not about stopping the virus. Babies admitted with RSV may need extra oxygen, according to the CDC. Hospital staff give it so the baby gets enough air while the airways are inflamed and clogged. Most babies admitted with RSV get better with this kind of supportive care within a few days. For a frightened parent, a typical stay means a few days of close monitoring and breathing support, and then the baby goes home. Doctors and nurses spend those days keeping the baby comfortable and well supplied with air while the baby's body fights off the infection.

That reassurance has limits. Recovery within a few days describes most babies who improve with supportive care, not every infant who is admitted. Some babies need far more support than an oxygen line, and doctors may put them on mechanical ventilation, with a machine doing the breathing for the baby. Ventilation is the most intensive care RSV can require. Families of newborns have the most reason to take that prospect seriously, with RSV putting 2–3 per 100 babies under 3 months old in the hospital every year. Those babies are also the ones with the least time to have built up any protection of their own.

Doctors can usually see a baby through RSV within days, but stays are common enough among newborns that the better outcome is a baby who never needs one, and immunization exists to make that happen.

Once a baby is sick with RSV, parents face one decision that matters more than any other: whether the baby can still be cared for at home. The CDC lists difficulty breathing as a sign that a baby needs emergency care, so a parent who sees a baby straining for air should get help right away. Worsening symptoms are the other signal. A baby whose cold-like illness keeps getting worse instead of easing is no longer following the course of an ordinary cold, and that change is the moment to have a doctor see the baby. The useful question at home is not how sick a baby looks at any one moment. It is whether the baby is laboring to breathe and whether the baby is getting sicker.

Parents who judge a baby's RSV by the thermometer can be misled in both directions. Many infants with RSV do not run a fever, so a normal temperature does not rule out the virus. The youngest babies also need a doctor at a much lower temperature than older children do. For a baby under 12 weeks, a reading of 100.4°F or above is a warning sign, according to the CDC. For older children, a fever counts as a warning sign only above 104°F, and only when medicine fails to bring it down. Parents might watch a temperature calmly in a toddler when the same reading in a newborn calls for a doctor.

A thermometer can tell parents when to worry about a newborn, but a normal reading can never tell them that the baby is fine.

A family can't treat RSV once a baby has it, but it can do a great deal before the virus arrives, and that work is simpler than many parents fear. The aim is to protect every infant, according to CDC guidance, and the CDC recommends two routes to get there: a vaccine for the mother during pregnancy or an antibody for the baby. For parents, the task is to pick the route that fits their timing, not to collect every shot available.

Families have no official best choice to hunt for. The two routes now cover three products, the maternal vaccine and two infant antibodies called nirsevimab and clesrovimab, and the CDC's vaccine advisers prefer none of them, according to CDC guidance. The official basis for choosing is practical: what the parents prefer, which product is available and when the birth falls relative to the RSV season. That leaves the decision in the parents' hands.

The maternal route depends on the due date. Pfizer $PFE's Abrysvo is given between 32 and 36 weeks of pregnancy and only from September through January, according to CDC guidance, so the mother has to be late in pregnancy during the fall and winter for the vaccine to be an option. Families expecting a baby in spring or summer will generally turn to the antibody instead. Mothers who choose the vaccine also accept a small trade-off. The vaccine was associated with a slight rise in high blood pressure disorders during pregnancy. That is worth raising with a doctor, but it is no reason to leave a baby unprotected.

Getting both protections is rare by design. "Most infants will not need both," the CDC said. The exceptions, according to CDC guidance, are babies whose mothers are immunocompromised or living with HIV, and those babies may get the antibody on top of the maternal vaccine. A mother with a weakened immune system may pass too few antibodies to her baby, and the extra shot is meant to make up that shortfall rather than repeat protection the baby already has.

The question to bring to the doctor is whether the baby is already covered, and if not, how to get protection in place before the season begins.

Why PIF may have placed Newcastle up for sale as Premier League takeover whispers revealed

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Senior Correspondent AUTHORITY Senior sports journalist with experience at GRV Media and VAVEL; University of Derby graduate. FOCUS Exclusive content, statistical analysis, and deep-dive reporting across the Breaking Media network. THE INSIGHT Max utilises a network of club and industry contacts to deliver verified, exclusive reporting and data-driven insight. He provides the intelligence behind the stories to ensure fans get the full picture.

PIF could be looking for a way out of Newcastle United behind the scenes.

A Saudi Arabia Public Investment Fund-backed takeover was sealed at St James' Park back in 2021.

There was mass hysteria in the days after the news as the days of Mike Ashley could finally be forgotten.

Things started well and ultimately culminated with the League Cup victory in the 2024/25 campaign.

Eddie Howe was representative of the new era, but with his departure, things just haven't been quite as enjoyable.

PIF could now have their eyes on a potential departure with reports emerging from throughout the Premier League.

There has been no shortage of annoyance from the Magpies owners towards financial rules in England and Europe.

They believe they have been put in place to hamper smaller sides from challenging the established elite sides.

Many owners are feeling the same frustrations and are looking to jump ship.

The Independent reported on Wednesday that "at least" eight Premier League clubs are either officially or unofficially up for sale.

There is said to be so few buyers that it has become difficult to find new investors.

The report details a "stagnation" in the market, partially due to "ongoing global uncertainty".

It also notes the "rumours" of PIF's willingness to sell Newcastle, but that is all they are, rumours.

Whether it be true or not, it feels as if they are now stuck where they are, so investment is necessary.

There may be Magpies fans who point towards the latest cash injection from the owners as reason that they are sticking around for the long term.

The Athletic reported earlier in November that £95million was injected into the North East club in October.

This was the 11th time they have done so, taking their funding to over £600m in almost exactly five years.

Although many may get excited by that news, it is a way to deal with losses and not breach SCR.

The £95m is largely due to cover operational costs, something which would be necessary whether they are planning to stay or go.

It could actually be a way of leaving the Toon in good stead for a fresh investor to come in and move from there.

It would be interesting to know how PIF see the future, but it seems that no one is 100 per cent sure in the Premier League.

Postpartum depression vs. baby blues: 10 things to know, per the NIMH

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Learn how postpartum depression differs from the baby blues, plus risk factors, treatment, and where mothers and families can find help.

Feeling low in the days after giving birth is so common that many families treat it as part of having a baby. The harder question is when that feeling stops being normal. Far more American mothers are being diagnosed with postpartum depression than a decade ago. The share of U.S. mothers reported to have it was 9.4% in 2010 and 19% in 2021, according to a JAMA Network Open study reported by The Associated Press. Even that higher count leaves many women out. Perinatal depression goes unrecognized in up to 50% of cases, according to StatPearls. The rising count shows that doctors are noticing more mothers, not that the problem is under control.

Many mothers miss the illness because they are watching for it in the wrong weeks. The familiar picture is the baby blues, a stretch of low mood that comes soon after delivery and then fades. Because of that timing, families come to think of postpartum sadness as something that begins with the birth. Yet about 50% of major depressive episodes in the postpartum period start while the woman is still pregnant, according to StatPearls. A mother waiting for a dip after the birth may already have an illness that started during her pregnancy. As a result, she and the people around her may watch the weeks after the birth closely and overlook the months before it. Telling a passing dip from an illness depends on knowing which signs matter and where to go when they appear, and a plain guide can supply exactly that.

The National Institute of Mental Health set out to explain perinatal depression, how to spot it, and where to get help. The federal mental-health agency wrote its guide in plain language and covered what family and friends can do, the rare emergency that calls for a hospital, and the treatments now on offer. A guide can tell a mother what to look for, but whether she gets help depends on what happens after she looks. The slides that follow set each part of the guide beside the evidence, showing where its advice holds, where newer research adds to it, and where care still falls short.

The low feeling that new and expecting mothers are told to ride out is sometimes a diagnosable illness. Perinatal depression belongs to the group of illnesses known as mood disorders, according to the National Institute of Mental Health. It is a medical condition rather than one of the ordinary emotions of pregnancy and new parenthood. Mood changes may point to postpartum depression when they become severe or last more than two weeks. Either one can be a warning sign, so a mother whose symptoms are severe has reason to speak up without waiting out a second week. The symptoms can also be mild, and a mother who is still getting through each day can have the illness. Coping is not the same as being well. The signs to watch are how severe the symptoms are and how long they last.

Calling perinatal depression an illness matters because leaving it untreated carries risks well beyond sadness. University of Utah Health reports that a mother whose perinatal depression goes untreated may struggle to connect with her baby or neglect the child, and that she may think about suicide or try to take her own life. In Utah, suicide is the leading cause of maternal mortality, which means more mothers there die by suicide than from any other cause. An illness that can end this way needs to be caught early, not waited out in the hope that it lifts.

Recognizing the illness is where help begins, not where it ends. A diagnosis does not mean a mother stays with the care she is offered, and 80% of diagnosed women don't act on the treatment advice they receive, according to University of Utah Health. Stigma is one of the barriers between these women and care. Mothers who see their symptoms as an ordinary rough patch can miss the illness, and mothers who feel ashamed may stay away from treatment even after a diagnosis.

Knowing what perinatal depression is gives a mother words for what she is feeling. Getting from those words to care is the harder step.

Most new mothers will recognize themselves in at least one warning sign of depression, and that match is a reason to talk with a provider, not a diagnosis. The National Institute of Mental Health says that a woman who notices even one symptom from its list should get checked by a health care provider. That advice leans toward caution rather than alarm. Many mothers will feel low in the first days, because the baby blues affect approximately 50% to 75% of new mothers, according to StatPearls. The sign that separates depression from the blues is one that involves the baby. The NIMH lists trouble bonding with the baby as a symptom, while the blues leave a mother's daily functioning untouched. A mother who can't connect with her child has gone past anything the blues explain. The useful question is whether her symptoms get in the way of her days and her baby, not whether she feels them at all.

Depression doesn't always show up as sadness, and its first signs can be physical. The NIMH counts physical aches with no clear cause among the symptoms. According to StatPearls, a diagnosis requires at least five symptoms together, and one of them must be depressed mood or a loss of interest and pleasure. Aches therefore matter most when they come with a flat, joyless mood, and neither one should be dismissed as tiredness. A single hard day or one stray symptom isn't depression, but several signs that pile up and stay can be. A mother and the people around her should watch for a pattern rather than a moment.

One set of symptoms needs no weighing at all. Postpartum psychosis brings delusions, hallucinations, mania, paranoia and confusion, and no family could mistake those signs for ordinary low mood. A mother with these symptoms needs emergency care, and the NIMH tells families to call 911 or take her to an emergency room. The condition is rare, at 1 to 2 per 1,000 pregnancies, according to StatPearls.

Knowing the signs means knowing which ones call for a talk with someone soon and which ones mean go now.

A mother who falls ill wants to know why, and the honest answer is that perinatal depression has no single cause, according to the National Institute of Mental Health. She has no one trigger to find, fix or regret. "A woman is not to blame or at fault for having perinatal depression: It is not caused by anything she has or has not done," the agency said. Doctors can use risk factors to decide which women need closer watching, but they can't use them to tell any one mother whether she will get sick.

Researchers are now examining medications as one possible contributor, and so far the evidence is thin. Researchers at Dongguan Maternal and Child Healthcare Hospital counted 643 cases of perinatal depression linked to a drug in safety reports, according to Frontiers. With only a few hundred cases, medications remain an early lead rather than a known cause. Hormonal contraception, one of the drug groups that appeared in those reports, is not a new suspect. Swedish researchers had already examined the contraceptive levonorgestrel and depression in 703,157 women, according to a 2023 study reported by Frontiers. Many women of childbearing age use contraception, and the link may partly reflect who takes these drugs rather than what the drugs do. The same caution applies to lurasidone, another drug flagged in the reports. The illness it is prescribed to treat, rather than the drug itself, may account for the link. A flagged medication is a question to ask a doctor, not a verdict.

Researchers can't yet draw a reliable portrait of which mothers face the most risk, because 48.1% of the reports left out the mother's age, according to Frontiers. Any profile built from these cases would rest on an incomplete record. Scientists can use such data to choose which questions to test, but they can't use it to sort mothers into safe and unsafe. The practical lesson is narrow: a mother's medications belong in the conversation about her mood, alongside everything else in her life. No single finding gives her reason to blame herself or her prescription.

Treatment for perinatal depression is a set of options that a mother can weigh with her provider, not one fixed path. Antidepressants are the standard treatment for keeping depression from returning, University of Colorado Boulder reports, and the National Institute of Mental Health says the risk that they cause birth defects when taken in pregnancy is very low. A pregnant woman deciding whether to stay on her medication can make that choice on the evidence rather than out of fear. Stigma, cost, transportation and a shortage of therapists still keep many mothers from care. The institute also lists zuranolone, which comes as a pill, among the treatments for the illness, and a mother can take a pill without first finding a therapist or a ride to one.

Women who have been depressed before have the most at stake, with rates of perinatal major depression as high as 77% among them, according to University of Colorado Boulder. For these mothers, keeping the illness from coming back is the main goal, and a free program called MMB for Moms gives them another way to pursue it. "What we do know now is that for patients looking for a non-pharmacologic alternative to mitigate their risk for recurrence of depression, this program helps," said Dr. Lee Cohen, the study's senior author and director of the Ammon-Pinizzotto Center for Women's Health at Massachusetts General Hospital. For a woman with that history, the best outcome is an episode that never begins, which is why prevention belongs among the treatments.

A mother who is already depressed needs a different kind of help. MMB for Moms is designed to prevent relapse rather than to treat active depression, University of Colorado Boulder reports, and its developers say it is not meant to "treat" the illness. A mother in crisis can reach the 988 suicide crisis line. Having more options helps only when a mother matches the option to where she is: still guarding against a return, or already in an episode.

A mother who suspects she is depressed does not have to begin with a specialist she must find, book and wait for. Many mothers assume help means a psychiatrist, and that assumption can stop them before they start. The National Institute of Mental Health counts primary care doctors among the providers who can help with the illness, alongside psychologists, psychiatrists and social workers, and its list is a sample rather than a complete roster. Her first step can be an appointment she already keeps. She can also search for care through an online treatment locator from the Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration. Clinics and doctors' offices keep business hours, though, and some mothers need to talk to someone sooner.

The fastest help is a phone line built for pregnant women and new mothers, and it is open at night, when a mother may be alone with the sense that something is wrong. The National Maternal Mental Health Hotline, which the NIMH recommends, is available 24/7, according to the CDC. Both agencies describe the hotline as free and confidential, so a mother worried about a bill or about who might find out can still reach out. Help is available in English and Spanish, and a Spanish-speaking mother can describe what she feels in her own language. Mothers can call or text the line at 1-833-TLC-MAMA (1-833-852-6262).

Help can also start with other mothers, before any clinician has made a diagnosis. Postpartum Support International is one support group the NIMH names, and the CDC lists it as a community resource. A mother who isn't ready to call a stranger or book an appointment can find women there who have been through the same thing. Each kind of help serves a different need: a doctor for treatment, the hotline for an urgent moment, and other mothers for company along the way.

Often the hardest part is deciding that help is needed, and after that a mother has more places to turn than she might expect.

The people around a depressed mother are not bystanders, and what they do can shape whether and how quickly she gets better. "Treatment is central to recovery," the National Institute of Mental Health said. A relative can help keep her moving toward that care. Getting her treated also matters for the rest of the household. The federal child health institute NICHD warns that older children may miss their mother's support while she is ill. A relative who pushes for treatment is looking out for the whole family, not only for her.

One concrete way to help is to take baby care and household chores off her hands, the tasks NICHD lists for mothers to request from family and friends. When other people handle the feedings and the housework, a struggling mother has time to rest and to seek treatment. Asking for that help is one more task for her, and a depressed mother may find it hard to do. Relatives and friends who offer a specific task without waiting to be asked spare her that step entirely.

Support works best when people arrange it before the baby arrives rather than piece it together in a crisis. Expectant mothers should work out during pregnancy who can help after the birth, according to NICHD. With that plan in place, help is ready when the weeks after the birth begin. A support plan belongs with the other preparations for a baby, as ordinary as buying a crib. No one should treat it as a rescue to organize only after something goes wrong.

The family's job starts before the birth and doesn't end until she is getting care.

A mother who wants to know more than the guide covers will have to look in several places, because the federal government spreads its information on this illness across several offices. The National Institute of Mental Health lists five resources for further reading, each from a different federal health office, so she needs to know which office to turn to. Some of those offices write about her condition as one form of depression in women. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, for example, covers depression among women in general rather than the months around a birth. The Office on Women's Health writes about postpartum depression, or PPD, specifically, and its advice speaks to a new mother's situation directly. Which page she reads first matters, because general guidance on depression and guidance written for pregnancy and new motherhood answer different questions.

A woman worried about her risk may also want to read about prevention, not only treatment. "PPD is very difficult to treat," Mi Hillefors, deputy director of the NIMH Division of Translational Research, said. The reading list includes guidance from the U.S. Preventive Services Task Force on preventive interventions for perinatal depression. When an illness is hard to treat once it starts, it makes sense to learn about prevention before symptoms appear. A woman who is pregnant and concerned about her risk can start reading during the pregnancy rather than wait until something feels wrong.

The reading list is most useful as a guide to where to turn next, and a mother gets the most from it by going straight to the pages written for her situation.

A mother who hears that clinical trials matter may assume that a better treatment is ready for whoever signs up. The National Institute of Mental Health lists clinical trials among the ways a mother can find help, but the medicines in those trials are still being tested. The drugmaker Lipocine is studying an investigational pill version of brexanolone for postpartum depression, and the drug has not been approved, according to Psychiatric Times. A mother who joins a study like this one helps answer whether a new treatment works. She does not receive a treatment that is already known to work, and that is the clearest way for any mother to think about enrolling.

For a mother caring for a newborn, the hope is that she could feel better quickly without leaving home. "Our goal is to develop a treatment that provides rapid symptom relief through a 48-hour oral course taken at home," Mahesh Patel, president and chief executive officer of Lipocine, said, as reported by Psychiatric Times. The researchers will measure success by how much the mothers' depression scores change 60 hours after treatment begins, according to Psychiatric Times. In other words, they want to learn whether women feel better within days. Rapid relief is the goal the study is designed to confirm or rule out, and it should not be read as a finding.

The answer will come from a small group of women. Lipocine plans to enroll about 120 participants in the study, which began in September 2026, according to Psychiatric Times. With results still to come, nobody can yet say whether the pill helps mothers with postpartum depression.

The existence of a trial is no proof that a drug helps, and the mothers who enroll are the ones who will help researchers find out.

For readers who want to go further, the National Institute of Mental Health points to a single resource: MedlinePlus, from the National Library of Medicine. The same resource is offered in Spanish, so Spanish-speaking families get the same next step as everyone else. Reading can help a mother put a name to what she feels, but nobody on the other side of a web page will check on her or connect her with care. Reaching a person matters more because the stakes go beyond her own health to her baby's.

A newborn whose mother shows signs of depression after the birth is much more likely to die in infancy. Newsweek reports that infant deaths ran at 7.2 per 1,000 births when mothers had postpartum depressive symptoms and at 2.0 per 1,000 births when they did not. The higher risk also held for babies born preterm, so early birth alone doesn't explain the difference. "In this study, we found a consistent association between postpartum depressive symptoms and infant mortality, but this study can't tell us whether this association is causal," said Rebecca Woofter, a postdoctoral associate at Rutgers School of Public Health and the study's lead author. The link gives a mother reason to act on symptoms early, for her baby's sake as well as her own. It doesn't show that a mother's depression causes her baby's death.

Newsweek reports that the study identified mothers with symptoms through the Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale, a screening tool. A high score on that tool marks a mother for a closer look rather than confirming depression. When a screening flags her, she should reach a person.

Once she has finished the reading, a mother's next step is a conversation with someone who can help.

Anyone may copy the words of the federal guide to perinatal depression and pass them along, and that freedom matters because recognizing the illness depends on the warning signs reaching whoever is close enough to notice them. The guide's written text belongs to the public domain, and no one has to seek approval before reusing it, according to the National Institute of Mental Health. A clinic, a support group or a worried relative can copy that text and give it to a new mother without asking anyone first. The only condition is that every copy credit the National Institute of Mental Health, part of the National Institutes of Health. A mother reading a photocopy can then see that the advice comes from the federal mental-health agency, not from an anonymous flyer. Family members and friends who want to help can use that permission in a concrete way and put the guide's words in her hands.

Anyone who shares the guide should first make sure the copy is current. The guide was last revised in 2023, and a printed copy is only as up to date as the day someone printed it. A clinic or relative preparing copies should check the NIMH website for the latest version rather than hand out an old one. Passing along the warning signs takes no one's approval, but the larger gap between a mother knowing the signs and actually getting care remains.

The guide is useful only when someone reads it, recognizes the signs in her and makes the call.
