The Most Expensive Artworks by Pablo Picasso Ever Sold at Auction

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Over the course of seven decades, Pablo Picasso maintained prolific periods of artistic output that cemented his status as the one of most coveted artists of all time. The modernist’s most prized paintings derive from various stages in his life: the early-career Rose and Blue periods and his groundbreaking experiments in Cubism, through his output in the years leading up to and during World War II up to the late 1960s, when he was already world famous.

Central to Picasso’s oeuvre are portraits of his various muses: Marie-Thérèse Walter, Dora Maar, Françoise Gilot, and Jacqueline Roque, each of whom the artist had a series of complicated affairs. These portraits, which range from sensual to tormented, have become some of the most recognizable in art history.

In the years since his death in 1973, Picasso’s stature as an artist elevated to the highest ranks, with some of his works going for more than $100 million in public auctions and reportedly at even higher sums in private deals. Currently, he remains the top-grossing artist at auction worldwide, raking in $245 million across 3,400 lots in 2020 alone. “He is in many ways the gold standard of the art market,” said dealer Larry Gagosian in an interview with ARTnews. “I think he’ll remain that way for the foreseeable future.” Below, a look at the top auction sales of works by the Spanish master.

Femme assise depicts French artist and model Fernande Olivier, who went with Picasso to a remote village in Spain during the summer of 1909. While there, she posed for a number of paintings. One of the few early Picasso Cubist works still in private hands, Femme assise sold for £43.2 million ($63.4 million) at a Sotheby’s London evening sale in 2016, where it exceeded the $43.1 million presale estimate. A telephone bidder won the painting, which had remained under the same roof for four decades. The anonymous seller had bought it in 1973 at Sotheby’s for £340,000 ($486,000). With many of Picasso’s early Cubist works in museum collections, Femme assise was a rarity on the open market at the time. “There hasn’t been a painting of this importance at auction in potentially a generation,” Helena Newman, Sotheby’s head of Impressionist and modern art in London, said at the time.

This vibrant red portrait of Dora Maar measures only 25⅝ by 21¼ inches. Auctioned by Christie’s New York in May 2015 at the house’s “Looking Forward to the Past” sale, it went for $67.4 million to an anonymous buyer, surpassing its $55 million high estimate.

Consigned by Las Vegas casino mogul and top collector Steve Wynn, the work was the third-highest sale of the night, which also saw the record-setting sale of Picasso’s Delacroix-inspired Les femmes d’Alger Version “O” for $179.4 million (see below). Before Buste de femme was acquired by Wynn, it had passed through the hands of the artist’s granddaughter Marina Picasso and Geneva modern art dealer Jan Krugier.

Bearing a second painting on the reverse, the rare double-sided canvas La gommeuse was painted by Picasso when he was only 19 years old. The artist created the picture while grieving the death of his friend and fellow artist Carles Casagemas, who had recently died by suicide—a tragedy that ushered in the Blue Period.

The work sold for $67.45 million (including buyer’s premium) at Sotheby’s New York in November 2015, though it was hammered in below its presale estimate of $60 million. The painting’s seller, American billionaire Bill Koch, had purchased it for $3 million in 1984 at auction. It wasn’t until 2000, while the work was being conserved, that a hidden portrait mocking Picasso’s friend and patron Pere Mañach was discovered on the reverse.

Before Koch owned it, La gommeuse had passed through the hands of esteemed French art dealer Ambroise Vollard, New York dealer Lucien Demotte, and Austrian-American film director Josef von Sternberg, who bought it in 1935 and sold it in 1949 at Sotheby’s for $3,600.

Picasso created his famed Guernica and The Weeping Woman in 1937 amid the tumultuous years of the Spanish Civil War. Painted the same year, Femme au béret et à la robe quadrillée features a more joyous image, that of his muse Marie-Thérèse Walter in a blue, red, and white outfit set against a purple background.

The painting made its auction debut during a Sotheby’s Impressionist and modern art sale in London. At that February 2018 sale, the work fetched £49.8 million (approximately $68.7 million); the winning bidder was Harry Smith, the London-based chairman of the art advisory firm Gurr Johns, who purchased it along with three other Picassos from the sale. It had changed hands only once before that auction: After Picasso’s death, his estate sold it to an unknown private collector.

Picasso’s Femme nue couchée depicts his muse Marie-Thérèse Walter reclining in a state of sensual abandon. The fluid brushwork, vivid contrasts of color, and radically distorted biomorphic forms lend the female body an almost sculptural presence. The Château de Boisgeloup in Normandy, where the painting was created, provided a studio for Picasso’s experiments with form and three-dimensional work.

The painting remained in Picasso’s collection until his death, then with his heirs until 2008, when it entered a private collection. Offered publicly for the first time, it headlined Sotheby’s Modern Evening Auction in New York on May 17, 2022. With an estimate reported at approximately $60 million, it achieved $67.5 million including fees; the hammer price was $58.5 million. The result made it the evening’s top lot.

Hidden from the public for almost 40 years, Dora Maar au chat portrays Picasso’s muse seated in a chair with a black cat on her shoulder. The painting was first acquired by Paris dealer Pierre Colle in 1946 and was later owned by Chicago collectors Leigh and Mary Block.

In 2006, Chicago’s Gidwitz family decided to auction the painting at Sotheby’s New York, where it sold for $95.2 million, almost twice the $50 million low estimate. Sotheby’s granted the family a $53 million guarantee in order to secure the painting, a rarity at the time.

At the evening sale, five bidders competed for the work. Reportedly among them were casino magnate Steve Wynn, Ohio retailer Leslie Wexner, and Microsoft cofounder Paul Allen. But the portrait of Maar went instead to an eager bidder in the room wielding a paddle with the number 1340, in an unusually visible move for a transaction of that level at a marquee auction.

The sale drew speculation as to the identity of the winning bidder, with many suspecting that he was an ultrawealthy collector from Russia. Ultimately, it was revealed that the anonymous floor bidder bought the work for the Georgian mining tycoon Bidzina Ivanishvili. New to the art world at the time, Ivanisvhili, who would go on to serve as Georgia’s prime minister, has a modern and contemporary art collection worth $1 billion, most of which he keeps in storage in London, with replicas of his masterpieces adorning his Tbilisi residence.

Leading Christie’s 20th-century art evening sale in New York in May 2020, Picasso’s Femme assise près d’une fenêtre (Marie-Thérèse) from 1932 sold for $103.4 million, almost double the $55 million pre-sale estimate. After a 19-minute long spar between six bidders, the painting finally sold to a bidder on the phone with Vanessa Fusco, Christie’s co-head of the 20th-century art evening sale, in a moment that proved the art market had returned in full force after a period of economic lag caused by the coronavirus pandemic.

Before its sale at Christie’s, it was last exhibited at Paris’s Picasso Museum showcase “Picasso 1932, année érotique” in 2017, alongside mega-collector Steven Cohen’s 1932 Picasso portrait Le Rêve, which he purchased for $155 million in 2013. The following year, Femme assis went to the Tate Modern in London for an exhibition titled “The EY Exhibition: Picasso 1932, Love Fame Tragedy.”

The work is notable both for its large size, and in its unusual depiction of Picasso’s muse, Marie-Thérèse Walter, who is often portrayed as sleeping or dreaming. Here, she is sitting upright in a chair, as if enthroned with her gaze directed at the viewer. The most striking aspect of the piece, Fusco told ARTnews ahead of the sale, is “the incredible presence of this woman and the agency which he’s given her, which you don’t always see in his depictions of her.”

Picasso’s painting of a seated Parisian teenager smoking a pipe and wearing a crown of roses is a signature work from the artist’s Rose Period and it is one of the works from this era that remains in private hands.

In May 2004, the work was offered at an evening sale in New York by Sotheby’s, alongside 33 other pieces from its owners Mr. and Mrs. John Hay Whitney to raise funds for the Greentree Foundation, the family’s charitable organization. The anonymous bidder, who purchased it for $104.1 million (well above the $70 million pre-sale estimate), beat out six others who were vying for it that evening.

The Whitneys had purchased it from a Zurich-based dealer for around $30,000 in 1950. The buyer is believed to have been Guido Barilla, the billionaire heir and chairman of the Barilla pasta company. When it sold in 2004, Garçon à la pipe became the most expensive ever sold at auction, beating out Van Gogh’s Portrait of Dr. Gachet (1890), which had sold for $82.5 million in 1990. Garçon à la pipe held that record until May 2010.

In May 2010, the star lot of an evening sale of Impressionist and modern art at Christie’s New York was Picasso’s Nude, Green Leaves and Bust, from his 1932 series of paintings of Marie-Thérèse Walter. During the sale, eight bidders competed for the work, driving the hammer well past the $70 million expectation. It ultimately sold for $106.5 million to a phone bidder.

Held in private hands for 60 years, the painting was sold by Los Angeles philanthropist Frances Lasker Brody, who had purchased it with her husband, Sidney F. Brody, from New York dealer Paul Rosenberg in January 1951 for $17,000.

The sale of Peggy and David Rockefeller’s art collection at Christie’s New York, which included top lots by Paul Gauguin, Edouard Manet, Henri Matisse, and Claude Monet, set a historic high for a single-owner sale in 2018. The piece that fetched the highest price was Picasso’s portrait Fillette a la corbeille fleurie, made during the artist’s Rose Period.

The work’s first owners were Leo and Gertrude Stein, the American siblings and early patrons of modern art, who bought it from a Paris dealer the year it was completed. After Gertrude Stein’s death, the painting was passed to her companion, Alice B. Toklas, whose estate sold it in 1968 to a syndicate of collectors associated with the Museum of Modern Art in New York. David Rockefeller then purchased it for himself.

When the painting sold for a staggering $115 million during the blockbuster Rockefeller auction in 2018, its price wasn’t the only reason the piece attracted attention. Featuring a nude prepubescent girl, wearing only a necklace and holding a basket of red flowers, the work generated controversy over Picasso’s sexualized image of a child, known among historians to be an underage flower seller and sex worker named Linda.

The work has long been considered a masterpiece, though. “She represents the themes that Picasso would wrestle with for his life—love, sex, beauty, tenderness, violence,” said Marc Porter, chairman of Christie’s Americas. Since the record sale of the work, criticism among scholars surrounding Picasso’s treatment of women has persisted. In 2021, protesters staged a demonstration outside Barcelona’s Picasso Museum in an attempt to bring attention to the issue.

In La Femme à la montre, Marie-Thérèse Walter appears elegantly dressed, wearing a wristwatch and sitting in an armchair against a blue background. The painting features bright colors, geometric shapes, and a harmonious composition. Her affair with Picasso remained secret for years because the artist was married to Olga Khokhlova. The wristwatch may symbolize the passage of time and the complexity of their romance.

In 1966, collector Ernst Beyeler purchased the painting directly from Picasso’s studio in Mougins, France. Two years later, Emily Fisher Landau acquired it. It remained a centerpiece of her collection for nearly 50 years, displayed above the fireplace in her Manhattan residence until her death in 2023. On November 8 of that year, Sotheby’s auctioned the painting in New York for $139.4 million, making it Picasso’s second-most expensive artwork sold at auction, after Les Femmes d’Alger (Version “O”).

In 1956, the legendary Picasso collector Victor Ganz put in an offer to buy all 15 pieces in Picasso’s Delacroix-inspired “Les Femmes d’Alger” series (1954–55) from Paris dealer Henry Kahnweiler for $212,500. In years to follow, Ganz kept just five from the group after selling the rest of them to recoup his purchase price; Version “O” was among the few he held on to.

Decades later, in 1997, Christie’s staged the blockbuster sale of Ganz’s prized Picasso collection in New York, where the piece went for $31.9 million to London dealer Libby Howie, who is said to have purchased it on behalf of a buyer in Europe. Nearly two decades later, at Christie’s New York in May 2015, Version “O” resurfaced on the market and set a record price of $179.4 million, making it the most expensive work to sell at auction at the time. Reported to have been purchased by the Qatari royal Al Thani family, that painting remains the most expensive Picasso ever to be auctioned.

FDA Initiates Labeling Changes for GLP-1s Due to Rare But Serious Eye Disease Risk

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

GLP-1 users have a new symptom to watch for: Sudden vision loss in one eye.

The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) announced on November 9 that it will require drugmakers to add information to the warning labels of Ozempic, Wegovy, Mounjaro, Zepbound and other GLP-1 medications about “a potential increased risk” of a rare eye condition called nonarteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (NAION).GLP-1 medications are approved to treat type 2 diabetes and to help people lose weight and keep it off.NAION joins a growing list of side effects linked to GLP-1s, adding a second eye warning to several labels.The U.S. labels for Ozempic, Rybelsus, Trulicity, Mounjaro, and Zepbound already warn about diabetic retinopathy, a diabetes-related eye disease, according to each drug’s prescribing information.The two conditions affect different parts of the eye. Diabetic retinopathy damages tiny blood vessels in the retina, the layer at the back of the eye that senses light, according to the National Eye Institute. NAION damages the optic nerve when it doesn’t get enough blood, according to the FDA’s announcement.Mihail Zilbermint, MD, director of endocrine hospitalists at Johns Hopkins, told Healthline he hasn’t seen NAION in his patients. He has seen vision worsen in some people after they start a GLP-1.“But we believe that’s probably related to rapid improvement in their sugar control, which can also be seen in someone who is starting insulin,” Zilbermint said.

A 2024 study published in JAMA Ophthalmology “initially triggered” the FDA’s review, the agency told Healthline.

The study found a higher risk of NAION in people prescribed semaglutide, the active ingredient in Ozempic and Wegovy, than in people prescribed other diabetes or weight-loss drugs.The authors wrote that more research is needed to determine whether semaglutide causes NAION.Joseph F. Rizzo III, MD, director of the Neuro-Ophthalmology Service at Mass Eye and Ear in Boston, served as the study’s corresponding author.

In a 2024 podcast, Rizzo said he and a resident saw three patients with NAION who were taking semaglutide for about a week.“And because NAION is a fairly uncommon disorder in the general population, the fact that we had three within a relatively short period of time led me to suspect that there was a higher risk of developing NAION in patients on semaglutide,” Rizzo said. The FDA analyzed clinical trials for multiple GLP-1 drugs and noted that NAION cases were reported with every GLP-1 on the market, according to its announcement. Those findings led the FDA to require the warning for the entire drug class, the agency told Healthline.

About 70% to 80% of people with NAION first notice vision loss when they wake up, according to StatPearls. The FDA listed these symptoms:

No treatment has been proven to reverse vision loss from NAION, according to StatPearls.

“Patients experiencing sudden, painless vision loss in one eye should contact their healthcare professional immediately and should not stop their medication without consulting their provider first,” the FDA told Healthline.

In the announcement, the agency told doctors to promptly refer patients with suspected NAION to an ophthalmologist.

The FDA listed these possible risk factors for NAION:

The FDA told Healthline that many people who take GLP-1s also have these conditions, which made reports of NAION in GLP-1 users “challenging” to interpret.

Across 126 clinical trials, the FDA found about 6 NAION cases per 100,000 people taking a GLP-1 for a year, and about 4 per 100,000 people taking a placebo or another diabetes drug for a year.In real-world data, NAION was more common with GLP-1s than with one type of diabetes pill, but the FDA found no significant increase compared with another type, the agency told Healthline.Zilbermint said patients who saw the news have worried about losing their vision. He tells them that “many of the serious harms, including the potential harm of NAION, are rare.”“The overall risk is still very small,” he said.

Regulators in other countries have added NAION warnings, mostly for semaglutide:

The FDA told Healthline the benefits of GLP-1s “continue to outweigh their risks when used as directed.”The FDA has also approved some GLP-1s to lower the risk of heart attack and stroke, reduce the risk of worsening kidney disease, and treat sleep apnea and a type of liver disease.Zilbermint said that “for most of my patients with obesity, diabetes and heart disease, the benefits clearly outweigh the risks.”“But we definitely have to decide patient by patient,” he added.

The labeling change applies to these medications, according to the FDA’s announcement:

As of publication, the FDA’s online label database did not yet show the new warning on the drugs’ labels.

The Airlines With The World's Newest Premium Economy Seats In 2026

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Around the world, premium economy is growing and more airlines are expected to increase their capacity in this class, although it can take years for an airline to fully transition to a new cabin type. Broadly speaking, premium economy is confined to widebody aircraft assigned to longer-haul routes.

However, there are exceptions like American Airlines' transcontinental A321XLRs and Emirates' widebodies that are used for short-haul routes. Widebodies generally fall into three categories as far as rolling out a new seating class is concerned. One category is new aircraft being delivered, which are typically received from the manufacturer with the latest airline cabins.

Another group of aircraft is those already in service with years of service ahead of them. These are retrofitted as they are cycled through their scheduled maintenance. Finally, there are older aircraft in service that are scheduled to retire that an airline may not bother retrofitting.

Originally, all commercial fares were effectively first class, as flying was only something the elite could afford to do. Over time, economy class emerged, and then in the 1970s, business class emerged as a bridge between first and economy. For many business class passengers, first class was excessively expensive, while economy was not befitting of business representatives on business trips.

The class also introduced the notion of working on the aircraft. As air travel became mainstream and everyone could fly, flying lost its prestige status symbol. Private jets may be the domain of the wealthy, but not commercial flying, and no longer did passengers dress up for the occasion and treat it as something of a first-class voyage.

Without the status of the past, the first class became seen as excessively expensive, while also being squeezed by an improving business class. This has led to the decline of first class around the world. Meanwhile, pressures mounted in two directions. One was the increased demand for ultra-cheap and utilitarian flights, which gave rise to low-cost and ultra-low-cost carriers.

Another was the demand for a class better than economy, but not as prohibitively expensive as business class. This led to the rise of premium economy as a 'sweet spot' between affordability and comfort. It fits today's mainline market, where flying needs to be comfortable and affordable, but not overly lavish.

Premium economy is the youngest of the four classes, and many airlines were reluctant to introduce it. Some airlines saw it as a threat that would undermine their profitable business class, as premium economy works if it gets economy-class passengers to upgrade, but it is counterproductive if it convinces business-class passengers to downgrade.

Some mainline carriers, like Emirates, were also concerned about how it would affect their brand image as a luxury mainline airline. Now, however, that dam has burst, and airlines are rushing to retrofit their aircraft with premium economy. Emirates CEO Tim Clark said that "our Premium Economy product was carefully developed in keeping with Emirates’ brand positioning as a full-service airline of the highest quality."

Industry Standards For Premium Economy (Per Emirates)

Cost above the economy (varies regionally)

Business cost above premium (varies regionally)

He has also described the demand for Emirates' premium economy as "tremendous" and "shocking" while noting he could sell the seats multiple times. Emirates is a great example of a holdout that has now fully embraced the class.

In 2026, Emirates has not been introducing a new premium economy, as it already exists, but it is rolling it out for more and more routes as aircraft are retrofitted, such as Tokyo to Cairo via Dubai. Lufthansa has gone so far as to call it a "money-generating machine" and claim it generates 33% more per square foot than economy class and 6% more than business class.

While Emirates has its own official Premium Economy, both Qatar and Etihad have their own unique approach to an elevated economy experience.

United Airlines is upgrading its Boeing 787-9's international cabins with a refreshed premium economy class as part of its broader 'Elevated Interior' launch. In May 2025, the carrier said that "United expects to take delivery of the first 787-9 with the Elevated interior before the end of 2025, ​​​​​ with the first international passenger flights planned in 2026 from San Francisco to Singapore and San Francisco to London."

American Airlines' new premium economy debuted in late 2025 on its new Being 787-9 Dreamliners (789P) and A321XLR transcontinental aircraft, while existing 777-300ERs are being retrofitted with the new seats. This is part of American's cabin refresh, and it is the last US-based airline to finally ditch first class, while it rebalances with more premium economy options.

Delta Air Lines is enhancing its premium economy class at the expense of economy class seats, while JetBlue is introducing its Junior Mint seats in 2026. While not typically classified as traditional premium economy, Junior Mint is still presented as a bridge between economy and business.

One holdout appeared to be Alaska Airlines. Its Hawaiian-bound Boeing 787-9 Dreamliners were initially only configured with business and economy class. However, it announced in October that, from 2028, 787s, A330s, and select 737 MAX 10s will get "international-grade premium economy with 38 inches of pitch, leg and calf rests and 16-inch 4K screens."

In Europe, Virgin Atlantic is taking delivery of new A330neo aircraft with expanded premium cabins (56). Elsewhere, Lufthansa is now accepting its new Boeing 787 Dreamliners after delays with the new Allegris cabins, while more A350-900s with Allegris premium economy cabins are delivered. Older Lufthansa aircraft, like 747-8s, are to be retrofitted.

In December 2025, Business Travel News Europe reported that "TAP Air Portugal in summer 2026 will introduce a new premium economy cabin on long-haul flights for its Airbus A330 and A321LR aircraft." It added that this will include 12 seats in three rows of four on these narrowbody aircraft. British Airways is also in the process of refreshing its cabin interiors, including its premium economy seats.

By now, the vast majority of major European full-service airlines with significant long-haul networks have adopted premium economy. However, there are a few holdouts, most notably Ireland's Aer Lingus, Spain's Air Europa, and, until now, Turkish Airlines. Turkish Airlines is, however, relaunching its premium economy after a lengthy hiatus on long-haul flights in 2028.

The refreshed premium economy seats are more comfortable, aesthetically pleasing, and technologically advanced than older seats.

EVA Air unveiled its fourth-generation premium economy seats in early 2025. As a side point, EVA Air is widely credited for having launched the world's first true premium economy class in 1992, which, at the time, was called Evergreen Deluxe. Singapore Airlines and Air France are also among the airlines rolling out refreshed premium economy seats in 2026.

Philippine Airlines has unveiled its first Airbus A350-1000 with a premium economy cabin, and more of these aircraft have arrived in 2026. As stated, one of the biggest stories for premium economy is Emirates, which is estimated to be adding 10 more premium economy routes, bringing the total to over 84. It is rolling out premium across its refreshed Boeing 777s and A380s, while, as a first, its new A350-900s are not being delivered with first class seats in favor of premium economy.

Air New Zealand is among the airlines doubling down on premium economy, introducing increased premium economy capacity on its retrofitted Boeing 787 Dreamliners from late 2025 into 2026. The carrier stated in mid-2025 that "customers travelling to North America will have access to more than 34,000 additional seats, alongside a boost of 20,500 premium seats across the wider long-haul network."

Elsewhere, Thai Airways has plans to add a new premium economy cabin to its 777-300ERs and incoming 787-9s starting around 2027. Meanwhile, new premium economy announcements in 2025 included LATAM saying it would introduce a new Premium Comfort cabin on its Boeing 787-8 and 787-9 starting in 2027.

Premium economy is now the fastest-growing class, having grown by 8% in recent years, ahead of economy's 5.9% growth. In 2027, Germany's leisure carrier, Discover Airlines, is introducing a premium economy cabin known as 'Ocean Blue' on its A330 widebody fleet. flydubai is also adding premium economy for the first time on its new Boeing 787 Dreamliners in 2027.

In contrast, first class seating is down by around 40% compared with 2019. In Q4 of 2025, Delta Air Lines reached a new milestone, as premium cabin revenue exceeded economy revenue. The final quarter saw main cabin revenues fall by 7% from $6 billion to $5.6 billion.

Meanwhile, premium revenues jumped by almost the same amount for the quarter ($473 million), from $5.2 billion in 2024 to $5.7 billion in 2025. Overall, the full-year main economy cabin revenues exceeded those of the premium economy cabin, but it seems that 2025 was the tipping point for Delta.

The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

According to XRPScan, XRP Ledger payments volume rises again, reaching 1.16 billion XRP on November 9.

XRP's payment volume is up 207.4% over 24 hours, sitting far below its all-time high of 7 billion. Payments volume growth is substantial, but not record-breaking by any means, with the 30-day average at 580.6 million XRP.

It's important to keep in mind that it's coming due to the transition from the weekend trading session to Monday, and volume spikes are normal here. This isn't the first spike either: on October 30, the ledger moved around 1.8 billion XRP.

Late September was even busier, with payments volume reaching around 1.9 billion XRP on September 25, so the current growth is nothing new for the ledger. In October, things were essentially the opposite: volume was struggling to stay above 800 million XRP, and on October 24 it almost dropped to zero.

XRP price, however, is moving the other way, with the bear market getting worse over the past weeks and the asset losing a substantial part of its value.

Price is already struggling, trading at $1.09, and could be teetering on the edge of the $1 threshold, which, if broken, essentially enables a rapid decrease. XRP hasn't been below $1 since mid-August, when it dropped under that level for the first time since November 2024. If the threshold breaks this time, the drop most likely won't be a short one, as the bear market keeps aggravating.

Growing payments volume reflects growing fear among investors, who are most likely moving funds to exchanges in order to liquidate their coins. Volume is already going back down towards 300 million XRP on November 10, though the day isn't over yet, so the spike might be short-lived.

Payments volume rises, but so does the bear market pressure, with a possibility of a further downturn that can make things worse than they already are.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Moving everyday banking away from Fidelity for security. Vanguard Cash Plus or another bank?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Its not paranoia if they're really out to get you.
There are lots of low probability scenarios where using more than one institution could be important with the vast majority ultimately being temporary liquidity issues.
The main reason not to use several is the added complexity. You are multiplying the probability of ever having any issue but its 2 or 3 times a very low baseline probability so the absolute increased risk is low. That suggests to me to balance the risks and benefits have a backup for liquidity and dont make it too complex.

We keep a main brokerage with 3 months of spending in money market and no banking type account.We keep a secondary brokerage with another few months of short treasuries on autoroll; this also happens to have the workplace retirement stuff. We do not have apps to login to these on phone and both are secured with the best 2fa these brokerages support.
We have our main joint banking accounts with a regional bank and use the phone app to manage this.
Spending goes on credit cards that are on autopay. No debit cards.

Patriots keep losing ground in the AFC East in Week 9

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Coming off a Super Bowl berth, the New England Patriots entered the 2026 season with some high expectations. Those have not changed, but Sunday's 45-27 loss to the Green Bay Packers showed that meeting them might become even more of a challenge than initially anticipated.

What will happen over the coming weeks and months remains to be seen, of course, but for now let's focus on the concrete impact that defeat had on the standings.

The Patriots defending their AFC East title will be a challenge, even though they own the head-to-head advantage over the Bills courtesy of their Week 4 victory in Buffalo. However, what good is that advantage if you cannot get even in the standings? And with New England falling to 5-4 and the Bills winning their seventh game on Monday night against the Vikings, doing so will be tough.

Not only will the Patriots need the Bills to start dropping some games to overcome their current 2.5-point deficit, they themself also cannot allow many more slip-ups. Considering that five of their final eight opponents currently reside in either the AFC or NFC playoff pictures, that is far from a given.

Elsewhere in the AFC East Week 9 was business as usual. The Jets lost 35-12 to the Chiefs and are inching closer toward rebuild category once more, while the Dolphins — whose lone win this season came against New York — were once again unable to make enough plays to prevent a 19-9 loss to the Lions.

On the bubble: 8. Cleveland Browns (5-4), 9. Las Vegas Raiders (4-5), 10. Baltimore Ravens (4-5), 11. Houston Texans (3-5)

Not yet eliminated, but let's be real here: 12. New York Jets (2-7), 13. Indianapolis Colts (2-7), 14. Miami Dolphins (1-7), 15. Tennessee Titans (1-7), 16. Los Angeles Chargers (0-8)

Even though they were dominated on Sunday, the Patriots still remain in the driver's seat for a playoff spot in the AFC. They do have multiple advantages over the only other 5-4 team in the conference, the Browns.

New England's conference record is superior, while it also owns the strength of victory and strength of schedule tiebreakers at the moment. With no head-to-head matchup on the schedule, those tiebreakers might decide who makes it to the postseason when all is said and done.

Of course, there is still a long way to go before any conversations about the playoffs become serious. For the time being, though, the Patriots are still in control of their own destiny even if they ended up ceding more ground to the other wild card competitors.

On the bubble: 8. Seattle Seahawks (5-4), 9. Los Angeles Rams (5-4), 10. Dallas Cowboys (4-5), 11. New Orleans Saints (3-5), 12. Tampa Bay Buccaneers (3-6), 13. Atlanta Falcons (3-6)

Not yet eliminated, but let's be real here: 14. Arizona Cardinals (3-6), 15. Washington Commanders (2-5-1), 16. Philadelphia Eagles (2-7)

Losers of back-to-back games following their 6-0 start, the Vikings dropped all the way to the sixth seed in the NFC on Monday night. They are still well-positioned for a playoff push, however, as is the rest of their division: all three wild card spots are occupied by NFC North teams, as is the No. 2 seed.

The Seahawks and Rams, meanwhile, are both outside the playoff picture despite their winning records. The fourth seen, meanwhile, is occupied by a 3-5 Panthers team leading the worst division in football.

Managers can turn on staff when they're afraid of losing their own jobs

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Executives count on team leaders to keep everyone steady through cuts. But some bosses fear for their own roles and start belittling employees

A version of this article originally appeared in Quartz’s Leadership newsletter. Sign up here to get the latest leadership news and insights straight to your inbox.

Plenty of managers say little to their teams when job cuts are rumored. Some already know a layoff is coming and aren't allowed to discuss it. Others think the conversation will get out of hand, or would rather not face their own fear of being replaced, executive coach Dina Denham Smith explained in an article for Harvard Business Review last December.

With nothing to go on, staff tend to imagine the worst. Employees keep telling Smith, "I'm just putting my head down and laying low." They speak up less, avoid risks, and look after themselves before solving problems. An executive can easily mistake a quiet meeting for agreement when the people in the room are scared.

Many managers fear for their own jobs, too. If senior leaders leave them guessing about cuts, some start insisting on making every decision, belittling their teams in the process. Not only are staff worried about losing their jobs, but their bosses may begin to treat them worse too.

Executives tend to blame stress when managers snap at their team ahead of a layoff. Bank managers in China, for example, get more frustrated on days they're more afraid than usual for their jobs, according to a study by Fu Yang and colleagues in the Journal of Business Ethics. They also want to make more decisions themselves. The more managers care about being the one in charge, the likelier they are to turn rude and nasty with staff.

Managers are used to giving orders, according to Yang and colleagues. They stand to lose their say over a team, along with their paychecks, so they push their own staff around to prove they still run things. Executives can tell them to calm down, but the managers will still fear for their jobs.

Many managers get through rounds of cuts without bossing their staff around. They tend to say they bounce back quickly after hard times. Even on their worst days, they run their teams as usual, without insisting on making every decision or snapping at their employees. Before a layoff, a company needs its managers to take the news without turning on their staff. Yang and colleagues suggest checking how quickly job applicants and promotion candidates recover from setbacks before putting them in charge of a team. Current managers could get training to bounce back faster, with follow-ups every week or so.

Some managers take longer to get over bad news. They can still treat staff decently on days they're worried about their job. They do best when their minds stay on what's happening at work instead of racing ahead to what might come next. Two managers can hear the same news about a restructuring and run their teams very differently. The same manager can also handle it well one day and badly the next. A short meditation session or a daily guided recording of about 10 minutes may help managers keep their minds on what's in front of them, according to Yang and colleagues.

Staff tend to absorb some of their manager's strain, often without noticing, according to researchers at the University of Southern Denmark. Employees read the boss's face and voice for clues about how worried to be. Managers under strain may also plan the work less carefully and offer less help, making the job harder for everyone below them. So when senior leaders put dozens of middle managers under pressure at once, they're also putting dozens of teams under pressure.

Staff feel less strained when their bosses listen, fix practical problems, understand their jobs, and show them respect. How they're treated each day matters more to them than their managers' own stress. A CEO can't keep every manager calm through a round of cuts but can still ask managers to hear their staff out and fix the everyday problems that slow their teams down. Senior leaders can bring up both in every meeting with managers until the layoffs are over.

A manager can even calm a team down without any good news to give. Smith advises telling staff what's settled, what's still open, and when they'll hear more. A fixed update schedule helps too, even in weeks with nothing new to report. Smith said managers can admit they don't know yet whether jobs will be cut without making staff more afraid. Employees then spend less time swapping rumors, the source of much of their fear.

Smith reminds managers that their teams are "likely even further removed from information, power, and clarity than you are." With so little else to go on, staff take their cues from their manager. Often, the person they're watching is worried about losing a job, too.

Join 500,000+ readers who start their day with Quartz.

10 flu myths about vaccines, treatment and what influenza really is

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

These 10 flu myths cover vaccines, treatment and what the flu really is, from whether the flu shot gives you the flu to whether antibiotics can cure it

The word "flu" does a lot of work it was never meant to do. People use it for a 24-hour stomach bug, a heavy cold or almost any winter illness that keeps them home. Influenza is something more specific. It is a respiratory illness caused by influenza A and B viruses, and it infects the nose, throat and lungs.

That loose vocabulary is one reason flu myths persist. Another is the vaccine itself. Many shots are given once or a handful of times in a lifetime, but the flu vaccine is offered every year. Each new season brings a fresh round of questions about whether it is safe, whether it works and whether a given person needs it.

The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) recommends an annual flu vaccine for everyone aged six months and older, with few exceptions. That short line of guidance still leaves people with questions about the details.

Treatment generates its own confusion. Some people expect antibiotics for a fever and body aches, even though influenza is a viral infection. Others assume a healthy adult can shrug off the flu, or that a vaccine without a perfect record is not worth getting.

The 10 myths below are a selection, not a complete catalog. They cover three areas: what the flu actually is, what causes it and how it is treated, and how the vaccine works for different groups of people. Those groups include pregnant women and people with egg allergies. Each entry names the misconception and explains what is accurate instead. Where guidance applies, the entries name the organizations that set it, such as the CDC, the World Health Organization and the American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists.

Every slide is written to stand on its own. A reader who wants to know whether a flu shot can cause the flu, or how influenza differs from a common cold, can go straight to that entry. The answers draw on established medical guidance rather than anecdote.

A flu shot cannot give you influenza. Injected flu vaccines contain either inactivated virus or a single protein from the virus, and neither one can cause the illness.

An inactivated virus has been treated so that it can no longer cause infection. It still carries the features the immune system needs to recognize. That combination lets the vaccine prompt protection without making anyone sick.

A vaccine built around a single viral protein contains no whole virus at all. It holds only one piece of the virus for the immune system to learn.

Reactions after an injection are a separate matter. A sore arm, a low fever and aches can follow a flu shot. Those effects come from the immune system responding to the vaccine. They are not influenza.

The nasal spray vaccine, FluMist, works differently. It contains a weakened live virus rather than an inactivated one. That virus has been adapted to grow in the cool conditions of the nose, not in the warmer lungs.

FluMist can cause mild symptoms such as a runny nose. It cannot cause the flu.

The illness most people call "stomach flu" is usually not influenza. It is typically gastroenteritis caused by germs such as norovirus or rotavirus. Those germs are separate from the influenza viruses behind the flu.

Influenza is a respiratory illness. Its main features are respiratory rather than digestive. The shared nickname ties together two illnesses with different causes.

Everyday language drives much of the mix-up. In casual speech, "flu" can mean almost any short illness that leaves a person miserable for a few days. A bout of vomiting and diarrhea picks up the label because it feels flu-like. That happens even when no influenza virus is involved.

The nickname does not change the diagnosis. An illness caused by norovirus or rotavirus is gastroenteritis, whatever a household chooses to call it.

The label also blurs personal history. Someone who says they "had the flu" after vomiting and diarrhea has most likely had gastroenteritis. That illness reveals little about how influenza itself would affect them.

The line between the two is not perfectly sharp. Vomiting and diarrhea can occur with influenza. They show up more often in children than in adults. Even so, they are not the main features of the disease.

That pattern matters when reading symptoms. A child with influenza may vomit. The vomiting does not turn the illness into gastroenteritis, and the underlying infection is still influenza. An adult whose illness is mainly vomiting and diarrhea, with no respiratory symptoms, fits the profile of gastroenteritis more closely than influenza.

The naming confusion also shapes how people judge the flu shot. The influenza vaccine is made to protect against influenza viruses. It is not designed to prevent gastroenteritis from norovirus or rotavirus. A stomach bug in the weeks after a flu shot is a different infection. It is not a sign that the vaccine failed.

Influenza is caused by influenza A and B viruses, not by chilly air, a winter draft or stepping outside with wet hair. A person develops the flu only after one of those viruses gets into the body.

The myth sticks partly because of timing. In temperate regions, flu activity peaks in fall and winter. Those are also the months when people are most likely to feel cold. Seeing the two together makes the weather look like the cause.

The seasonal peak is tied to other factors. Flu surges in fall and winter are linked to conditions such as indoor crowding and drier air. In colder months, people spend more time inside and closer together. That gives an infected person more contact with others.

Feeling cold and catching influenza are separate events. Someone who walks outside in freezing weather with damp hair, and never encounters the virus, does not come down with the flu. Someone who stays warm indoors but spends time with an infected person can still get sick.

That distinction shapes what helps. A scarf, a hat or a dry head may make a winter day more comfortable. None of them stops an influenza virus from reaching a person who is exposed to it.

Antibiotics do nothing against the flu. These drugs act on bacteria, and influenza is caused by viruses. The medicines made to treat influenza are prescription antivirals, such as oseltamivir and baloxavir.

The gap comes down to the type of germ. Bacteria and viruses are different kinds of microbes. A drug that acts on bacteria has no effect on a virus, no matter how sick the patient feels.

Oseltamivir is sold under the brand name Tamiflu. Baloxavir is sold as Xofluza. The generic and brand names refer to the same drugs. Both target influenza itself, and a doctor has to prescribe them.

Timing shapes how well these antivirals work. They work best when a patient starts them within 48 hours of the first symptoms. That makes an early visit to a doctor more useful than waiting to see whether the illness gets worse.

The 48-hour mark is not a strict cutoff. Doctors may still prescribe an antiviral after that window to high-risk patients. They may also give one to people who are already in the hospital with influenza. In those cases, the decision rests with the treating doctor rather than the calendar.

Antibiotics do have a role in some flu cases. A bacterial complication can develop on top of the viral infection. Bacterial pneumonia is one example. In that situation, a doctor may prescribe an antibiotic.

Even then, the antibiotic treats the bacterial infection. It does not act on the influenza virus that came first. The flu and the complication call for two separate kinds of treatment. The reverse also holds. An antiviral such as oseltamivir targets influenza and will not clear a bacterial pneumonia.

For someone with flu symptoms, an antibiotic will not treat the illness. Asking a doctor whether an antiviral such as Tamiflu or Xofluza fits the case addresses the virus directly.

Being young, fit and rarely sick is not a reason to skip the flu shot. The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention recommends a flu vaccine every year for everyone aged six months and older, with few exceptions.

That guidance covers healthy adults on the same terms as everyone else. Good health is not one of the exceptions.

The myth rests on the idea that influenza threatens only people who are already frail. Healthy people can also become severely ill with the flu. A strong baseline of health does not guarantee a mild case.

The shot also matters for the people around a healthy adult. Someone who catches influenza can pass the virus on. Those on the receiving end may include infants, older adults and others whose age or health puts them at higher risk of severe illness.

A healthy adult may live with a baby, visit an older relative or work alongside someone at higher risk. In each case, that adult is one route the virus can take to reach them.

A flu shot from last season is not designed to protect you through this one. Influenza viruses change from year to year. For that reason, the strains included in flu vaccines are reviewed and updated before each new season.

That gradual change is called antigenic drift. As influenza A and B viruses circulate, they accumulate small changes. A vaccine built around earlier versions of the virus may not match the versions spreading now.

The result is a vaccine that is reformulated for each season rather than one product offered unchanged year after year.

The strain review takes place at a global level. The World Health Organization makes separate vaccine recommendations for the Northern Hemisphere and the Southern Hemisphere. In temperate regions, flu activity peaks in fall and winter. Those seasons arrive in different months on either side of the equator. Separate recommendations account for that difference.

Strain change is not the only reason an older shot falls short. Protection from flu vaccination also wanes over time. A shot given a year ago offers less protection now than it did in the weeks after it was given.

Both problems point the same way. A previous season's vaccine may target strains that no longer match the viruses in circulation. The immunity it produced has also faded. That combination is why the flu vaccine is treated as a seasonal shot rather than a one-time dose.

Influenza is not a stronger version of the common cold. The two illnesses are caused by different viruses, and the flu can lead to serious complications such as pneumonia.

Colds are commonly caused by rhinoviruses. The flu is caused by influenza viruses. Because the germs are different, a cold is not an early or mild stage of the flu. A bad cold does not turn into influenza.

The flu tends to start abruptly. Fever, body aches and exhaustion are typical early symptoms, and they often arrive together.

Complications set the two illnesses further apart. Influenza can lead to pneumonia, an infection of the lungs. Some people with the flu become sick enough to need care in a hospital.

Flu vaccine effectiveness varies by season, but the shot still cuts risk. The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) estimates that vaccination reduces the risk of flu illness by 40% to 60% in the overall population when circulating viruses are well matched to the vaccine.

That range describes a reduction in risk across a population. It compares vaccinated people with unvaccinated people. It does not mean that 40% to 60% of people who get the shot are protected while everyone else gets nothing from it.

The condition attached to the estimate matters. The range applies to seasons when the vaccine is well matched to the viruses that are spreading. In seasons with a weaker match, protection can differ.

The estimate also measures one specific outcome: flu illness. It describes how much vaccination lowers the chance of getting sick with influenza. It is not a promise that any one person will avoid infection.

A vaccinated person who still catches influenza is not evidence that the vaccine failed. One case cannot show what a vaccine did across a whole population. The CDC figure is built to answer a population question, not an individual one.

Preventing illness is not the only benefit. Vaccination can make illness milder in people who still get sick. Someone who catches influenza after vaccination may have a less severe course than they would have had without the shot.

The CDC and the American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists both recommend flu vaccination during pregnancy. That guidance runs against the myth that expectant mothers should wait until after the baby is born to get the shot.

The first reason concerns the mother. Pregnancy raises the risk of severe influenza. Putting off the vaccine until after delivery leaves her unprotected through the months when that risk applies.

The second reason concerns the baby. Infants younger than six months cannot receive a flu vaccine. Newborns therefore have no vaccine protection of their own during their first months of life.

Vaccination during pregnancy helps close that gap. The mother develops antibodies in response to the shot. Those antibodies are passed on to the baby during pregnancy. They help protect the infant until the child is old enough to be vaccinated.

The recommendation comes from two different kinds of institutions. The CDC is the national public health agency of the U.S. The American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists is the professional organization for obstetrician-gynecologists, the doctors who provide pregnancy care. Their shared position means public health guidance and specialist medical guidance point the same way.

One flu shot during pregnancy reaches two people at once. It addresses the mother's higher risk of severe flu. It also helps protect a baby who is too young for a vaccine of its own.

An egg allergy does not rule out a flu shot. The CDC advises that people with an egg allergy can receive any licensed, age-appropriate flu vaccine. No extra precautions are needed beyond standard vaccine safety practice.

That guidance covers every licensed flu vaccine, including those produced using eggs. A history of egg allergy is not, on its own, a reason to skip vaccination. Nor is it a reason to hunt for a particular brand.

The guidance places someone with an egg allergy under the same safety practice as any other vaccine recipient. An egg allergy does not determine which licensed vaccine that person can receive.

The age-appropriate part of the guidance still matters. Each flu vaccine is licensed for specific age groups. The right product depends on the age of the person receiving it, not on an egg allergy.

Egg-free options also exist. Flucelvax is a cell-based flu vaccine. Flublok is a recombinant flu vaccine. Neither is made with eggs.

Those products are an alternative, not a requirement. People who would rather avoid egg-based vaccines can ask about one of them, as long as it is licensed for their age group. Under the CDC guidance, a person with an egg allergy who receives an egg-based vaccine is also following the recommendation.
